Table 1
The IASB and the IFRS Conceptual Framework: Governance, Reporting Principles, and Application
1. Q: What is the relationship between the IASB and the Conceptual Framework in developing and applying IFRS Standards?
A: Show answer
The International Accounting Standards Board (IASB) develops and issues IFRS Accounting Standards. The Conceptual Framework for Financial Reporting supplies the underlying concepts that guide the Board when developing standards and help preparers and users understand financial reporting. It explains the objective of general-purpose reporting, the qualities that make information useful, the elements of financial statements, and principles for recognition, measurement, presentation and disclosure. The Framework is not a substitute for an applicable Standard: preparers apply the specific IFRS requirements that govern a transaction. Together, standards and concepts provide a structured basis for consistent reporting while leaving room for professional judgement where standards require choices or do not directly address an issue.

Study instruction:
- "Map the IASB's standard-setting role against the Conceptual Framework's role for standard setters, preparers and users."

Key point:
- The IASB issues Standards; the Conceptual Framework supplies their conceptual foundation.
- Apply the specific IFRS Standard when it governs the transaction.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation: Our structure
2. Q: How did international accounting standard setting develop from the IASC to the IASB?
A: Show answer
The International Accounting Standards Committee (IASC) was proposed in 1972 and formed in 1973 by professional accountancy bodies from nine countries. It developed a core set of International Accounting Standards (IAS Standards). A review of its operations in the late 1990s led to restructuring, and the International Accounting Standards Board began operating in 2001 within the new IFRS Foundation structure. The IASB initially adopted existing IAS Standards, with some modifications, and subsequently issued new or revised requirements under the IFRS name. This history explains why today's body of IFRS Accounting Standards includes both older IAS Standards and newer IFRS Standards; the prefix does not by itself determine whether a standard remains authoritative.

Study instruction:
- "Create a short timeline covering the IASC's formation, the restructuring decision, the IASB's establishment and the continuation of IAS Standards."

Key point:
- The IASB succeeded the IASC as the full-time international standard setter in 2001.
- The IFRS body of literature includes both IAS Standards and IFRS Standards.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation: Our structure
3. Q: What weaknesses in the IASC prompted the restructuring of international standard setting?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies weak relationships with national standard setters, insufficient convergence with standards used in major jurisdictions, a part-time board, and limited resources and technical support. These weaknesses made it harder for the IASC to develop standards that were broadly accepted, technically robust and responsive to increasingly complex reporting issues. The restructuring sought a smaller, full-time IASB supported by professional staff and a more formal governance and oversight system. The lesson is institutional: global standards need more than agreement on technical wording. They also require credible governance, adequate resources, transparent processes and meaningful engagement with national standard setters and affected stakeholders.

Study instruction:
- "For each identified weakness, explain how the revised institutional model was intended to improve capacity, legitimacy or convergence."

Key point:
- The restructuring addressed capacity, representation and convergence concerns.
- Credible global standards depend on governance and resources as well as technical expertise.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation: Our structure
4. Q: Why does the term IFRS Standards include both IAS Standards and IFRS Standards?
A: Show answer
The IASC issued International Accounting Standards before the IASB was established. When the IASB took over, it initially adopted those IAS Standards, sometimes with modifications. As the IASB revised older requirements or issued new ones, the resulting standards were generally given the IFRS designation. Consequently, the collective body of IFRS Accounting Standards includes standards titled IAS and standards titled IFRS. A preparer should therefore not assume that an IAS-prefixed standard is obsolete or that only IFRS-prefixed standards matter. The relevant question is whether the standard remains applicable and what its current requirements say for the transaction being reported.

Study instruction:
- "Select examples of one IAS Standard and one IFRS Standard and explain why both belong to the IFRS literature."

Key point:
- IAS and IFRS are both part of the body of IFRS Accounting Standards.
- Check a Standard's current applicability rather than judging it by its prefix.
5. Q: How is the IFRS Foundation's governance structure organised?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a three-level structure. The standard-setting level includes the IASB, the IFRS Interpretations Committee and the International Sustainability Standards Board (ISSB). The IFRS Foundation Trustees appoint and oversee the members of the Boards and committees and provide governance oversight. The IFRS Foundation Monitoring Board provides public accountability for the Trustees. Advisory bodies and working groups support consultation and technical input but are not substitutes for the Board's standard-setting authority. Understanding the structure helps distinguish who develops requirements, who provides oversight, who advises and who is responsible for public accountability.

Study instruction:
- "Draw the governance structure and label standard setting, trustee oversight, public accountability and advisory input separately."

Key point:
- The IASB and related boards perform standard-setting work within a wider governance structure.
- Trustees and the Monitoring Board provide oversight and accountability.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation: Our structure
6. Q: What is the IASB's principal responsibility?
A: Show answer
The IASB is the independent standard-setting board responsible for publishing IFRS Accounting Standards. Its work involves identifying reporting problems, researching possible solutions, consulting stakeholders, deliberating publicly and issuing or amending requirements through due process. The Board's purpose is not to prepare an individual entity's financial statements or to enforce every entity's compliance. Preparers apply the Standards, auditors evaluate whether reporting complies with the applicable requirements, and regulators or other authorities may have enforcement responsibilities under their own mandates. Keeping these roles distinct prevents confusion between developing a standard and applying or enforcing it.

Study instruction:
- "Distinguish the IASB's standard-setting responsibility from the responsibilities of preparers, auditors and regulators."

Key point:
- The IASB develops and issues Standards; it does not prepare each reporting entity's accounts.
- Application and enforcement involve other participants in the reporting system.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation: Our structure
7. Q: What does the IFRS Interpretations Committee do when an accounting issue is not clearly addressed?
A: Show answer
The IFRS Interpretations Committee considers newly identified financial reporting issues that are not specifically addressed by existing IFRS requirements, as well as situations in which unsatisfactory or conflicting interpretations have emerged or may emerge. It seeks consensus on appropriate accounting treatment and can recommend that the IASB amend a Standard, issue an Interpretation, or take no further standard-setting action. Its work helps address application questions and supports consistent interpretation across entities and jurisdictions. Preparers should examine relevant Interpretations and agenda decisions when analysing a difficult issue rather than relying only on a broad reading of a Standard's title or summary.

Study instruction:
- "Trace how an identified application issue can move from committee analysis to an Interpretation, an amendment proposal or an agenda decision."

Key point:
- The Interpretations Committee addresses application issues and inconsistent interpretations.
- Its work supports consistent application of IFRS requirements.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation: Our structure
8. Q: How should preparers understand IFRIC Interpretations and agenda decisions?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that Interpretations approved by, or not objected to by, the IASB are regarded as forming part of the IFRS requirements and have status equivalent to Standards for compliance purposes. An agenda decision may explain why the Committee does not recommend a new Interpretation or amendment, while still describing how existing requirements apply to the fact pattern. Preparers should therefore read the reasoning and identify the relevant Standard paragraphs rather than treating an agenda decision as an unrelated commentary. The precise legal or regulatory effect can depend on the applicable jurisdiction, so entities should also consider local endorsement and effective-date arrangements.

Study instruction:
- "Review an Interpretation or agenda decision and identify the underlying Standard requirements it explains and any jurisdictional implementation considerations."

Key point:
- Interpretations and agenda decisions can materially affect how existing Standards are applied.
- Read the reasoning and check applicable local endorsement requirements.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation: Our structure
9. Q: How does the ISSB relate to the IASB?
A: Show answer
The ISSB was created in 2022 to develop IFRS Sustainability Disclosure Standards. The chapter distinguishes this sustainability disclosure remit from the IASB's responsibility for IFRS Accounting Standards. It also notes that the two Boards are expected to work together where sustainability-related uncertainties may affect financial statements. For example, sustainability matters can influence estimates, impairment assessments, useful lives, provisions or risk disclosures under accounting requirements even when the sustainability disclosure standard itself is outside the scope of a particular accounting analysis. The distinction helps users understand that sustainability disclosures and financial statements are related reporting streams, but they are not interchangeable.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a two-column role comparison for the IASB and ISSB, then identify one sustainability matter that could affect accounting estimates."

Key point:
- The IASB sets accounting standards; the ISSB sets sustainability disclosure standards.
- The Boards coordinate where sustainability matters affect financial reporting.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation: Our structure
10. Q: What are the responsibilities of the IFRS Foundation Trustees and Monitoring Board?
A: Show answer
The Trustees appoint and oversee the members of the IASB, ISSB and IFRS Interpretations Committee and are responsible for the Foundation's governance arrangements. The chapter describes the Trustees as geographically and professionally diverse. The Monitoring Board provides public accountability for the Trustees. This arrangement separates technical standard-setting from the governance and accountability mechanisms that support the legitimacy of the process. The Trustees do not replace the IASB in deciding the detailed accounting requirements of a Standard, and the Monitoring Board does not prepare technical interpretations. Each level has a distinct role in maintaining a credible standard-setting institution.

Study instruction:
- "Explain which body appoints standard setters, which body provides public accountability and which body makes technical accounting decisions."

Key point:
- Trustees oversee appointments and governance; the Monitoring Board provides public accountability.
- Technical standard-setting remains the IASB's responsibility.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation: Our structure
11. Q: How do advisory bodies and working groups contribute to IFRS standard setting?
A: Show answer
The IFRS Advisory Council provides strategic advice to the IASB, ISSB and Trustees. Other bodies, such as the Capital Markets Advisory Committee, Emerging Economies Group, Global Preparers Forum and SME-related groups, provide channels for engagement with stakeholders who have different experiences and needs. The IASB may also establish project-specific working groups to obtain specialist knowledge, as illustrated by the Insurance Working Group. These groups improve the evidence and perspectives available to standard setters, but they do not independently issue IFRS Standards. Their value lies in identifying practical consequences, implementation concerns and unintended effects before requirements are finalised.

Study instruction:
- "For a proposed accounting change, identify which preparers, users, investors, smaller entities and technical specialists should be consulted."

Key point:
- Advisory bodies broaden stakeholder input and technical insight.
- The IASB retains responsibility for issuing the final Standard.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation: Our structure
12. Q: Why does the IFRS Foundation use a formal due process for developing Standards?
A: Show answer
A formal due process is intended to make standard setting thorough, inclusive and transparent. Reporting requirements can affect entities, investors, lenders, auditors, regulators and markets across jurisdictions, so a standard should not be developed solely from an internal technical preference. Public consultation allows the IASB to test proposals, identify operational consequences, receive evidence and explain how it has considered stakeholder feedback. Transparency also makes the process more accountable and can strengthen acceptance and adoption. Due process does not guarantee that every stakeholder will agree with the final result; it provides a disciplined method for reaching and explaining a decision.

Study instruction:
- "Explain how research, consultation, public deliberation and documented feedback each contribute to the legitimacy of a new Standard."

Key point:
- Due process supports quality, transparency and broad stakeholder participation.
- Consultation informs decisions but does not require unanimous agreement.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation: What is due process and why does it matter?
- IFRS Foundation: Due Process Handbook
13. Q: What are the main stages in the IASB's standard-setting process?
A: Show answer
The chapter summarises a sequence beginning with research into perceived shortcomings and possible improvements. A matter considered important enough may be added to the IASB's work plan after the required governance consultation and public Board consideration. The Board then develops proposals, may establish a consultative group, deliberates in public meetings and publishes an exposure draft for comment. After the comment period, it analyses feedback, redeliberates and decides whether further consultation or a revised exposure draft is needed. If the project proceeds, a final draft is balloted and published when the required voting threshold is achieved. Major new or amended Standards may also receive supporting explanatory material.

Study instruction:
- "Reconstruct the sequence from research through work-plan addition, exposure draft, redeliberation, ballot and publication."

Key point:
- Standard setting moves from research to proposal, consultation, redeliberation and final publication.
- Public feedback can change a proposal before it becomes a requirement.

Source links:
- IAS Plus: IASB due process
- IFRS Foundation: Due Process Handbook
14. Q: Why are exposure drafts and public comment periods important?
A: Show answer
An exposure draft makes the IASB's proposed requirements available for scrutiny before finalisation. Stakeholders can identify ambiguous wording, implementation costs, inconsistent outcomes, data limitations and unintended consequences that may not be apparent to the Board during initial research. The IASB considers the feedback and may amend the proposal, undertake additional consultation or, in exceptional circumstances, reconsider whether the project should continue. A comment letter should therefore explain the practical facts, affected transactions, evidence and suggested alternatives rather than simply express support or opposition. The quality of feedback depends on clear analysis and credible examples.

Study instruction:
- "Draft a comment-letter outline that identifies the proposed requirement, operational impact, supporting evidence and a practicable alternative."

Key point:
- Exposure drafts allow stakeholders to test proposals before they become final requirements.
- Evidence-based feedback can lead to redeliberation or revised proposals.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation: What is due process and why does it matter?
- IFRS Foundation: Due Process Handbook
15. Q: What is the purpose of voting thresholds and post-implementation reviews?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a supermajority vote as necessary for publication of a final Standard, with the required number depending on the Board's membership at the time. Voting provides a formal decision point after research, consultation and redeliberation. For a new Standard or a major amendment, a post-implementation review is generally undertaken roughly two and a half to three years after the requirements become effective, although timing may be deferred in some circumstances. The review considers whether the requirements are working as intended and whether unexpected costs, application problems or reporting outcomes have emerged. It is a feedback mechanism, not an automatic promise that a Standard will be changed.

Study instruction:
- "Identify the approval milestone for a final Standard and explain what evidence a post-implementation review should examine."

Key point:
- A final Standard requires the applicable voting threshold.
- Post-implementation reviews assess real-world effects after implementation.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation: Due Process Handbook
- IAS Plus: IASB due process
16. Q: What is the purpose of the Conceptual Framework for Financial Reporting?
A: Show answer
The Conceptual Framework sets out the concepts underlying general-purpose financial reporting. It assists the IASB in developing internally coherent Standards, helps preparers develop an accounting policy when no specific Standard applies or when a Standard permits a policy choice, and helps users understand and interpret financial reporting requirements. It provides a common language for discussing the objective of reporting, information quality, financial statement elements, recognition, derecognition, measurement, presentation and disclosure. It is therefore a foundation for reasoning, not a checklist that replaces detailed requirements. Its usefulness is greatest when users understand both its concepts and the limits on its authority.

Study instruction:
- "Explain the Framework's three main user groups and give one example of how each group uses it."

Key point:
- The Framework supports standard setters, preparers and users of financial statements.
- It supplies concepts for reasoning where detailed Standards do not settle every question.
17. Q: Is the Conceptual Framework itself an IFRS Accounting Standard?
A: Show answer
No. The chapter expressly distinguishes the Conceptual Framework from an IFRS Standard. It guides the IASB and helps preparers develop accounting policies when there is no directly applicable Standard or when an existing Standard allows a choice. If a specific IFRS Standard addresses the transaction, its requirements take precedence over a conflicting or older Framework concept. This distinction is especially important because some Standards were developed before the 2018 revision of the Framework and may not align perfectly with its current wording. A preparer should first identify the applicable Standard and use the Framework only within the role it is permitted to play.

Study instruction:
- "Work through a case in which a specific Standard and a general Framework principle appear different, and explain why the Standard governs."

Key point:
- The Conceptual Framework is not itself an IFRS Standard.
- Specific applicable Standard requirements take precedence over general Framework concepts.
18. Q: What are the eight chapters of the revised Conceptual Framework?
A: Show answer
The revised Framework is organised around eight areas: the objective of general-purpose financial reporting; qualitative characteristics of useful financial information; financial statements and the reporting entity; the elements of financial statements; recognition and derecognition; measurement; presentation and disclosure; and concepts of capital and capital maintenance. These areas form a logical progression. The objective explains what reporting is intended to achieve; qualitative characteristics define useful information; the element definitions and recognition principles determine what enters the statements; measurement determines amounts; presentation and disclosure communicate the information; and capital maintenance explains one basis for determining profit. The chapter uses these areas as the foundation for applying IFRS concepts.

Study instruction:
- "Memorise the eight chapter topics and map each one to a reporting decision that a preparer must make."

Key point:
- The Framework moves from reporting objectives through elements, recognition, measurement and communication.
- Capital maintenance connects the measurement of capital with the determination of profit.
19. Q: What is the objective of general-purpose financial reporting?
A: Show answer
The objective is to provide financial information about a reporting entity that is useful to existing and potential investors, lenders and other creditors when they decide whether to provide resources to the entity. Those decisions include buying, selling or holding equity and debt instruments, providing or settling loans and other credit, and exercising rights to vote on or otherwise influence management's use of economic resources. The objective is decision usefulness, not simply compliance with a reporting calendar or production of a large volume of data. Information should help users assess the entity's financial position, performance and changes in financial position.

Study instruction:
- "For a sample financial statement item, identify how it could influence an investor's, lender's or creditor's resource-allocation decision."

Key point:
- General-purpose reporting is designed to support resource-allocation decisions.
- The principal user groups identified are investors, lenders and other creditors.
20. Q: How do general-purpose financial statements differ from special-purpose reports?
A: Show answer
General-purpose financial statements address common information needs of users who cannot require an entity to prepare a report tailored to their individual requirements. A tax authority or banking supervisor, for example, may have statutory powers to demand specific information for its own purposes. Such special-purpose information may be useful, but it is not the primary objective described by the Conceptual Framework. A preparer should therefore distinguish the purpose and user group of each report. A general-purpose statement should not be redesigned solely to answer one user's unique question if doing so would obscure the information needs of the wider group.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a published general-purpose financial statement with a tax or regulatory return and identify the different users and purposes."

Key point:
- General-purpose reports meet common information needs across a user group.
- Special-purpose reports address tailored information demands of particular authorities or users.
21. Q: What are the fundamental and enhancing qualitative characteristics of useful financial information?
A: Show answer
The two fundamental qualitative characteristics are relevance and faithful representation. Information is relevant when it can make a difference to a user's decision, and it is faithfully represented when the depiction is complete, neutral and free from material error. Four enhancing characteristics make useful information more useful: comparability, verifiability, timeliness and understandability. The enhancing qualities cannot rescue information that is irrelevant or fundamentally unfaithful, but they improve the usefulness of information that already meets the fundamental qualities. The characteristics should be considered together when designing accounting policies, estimates and disclosures.

Study instruction:
- "Classify examples of financial information under the two fundamental and four enhancing characteristics and explain any weakness."

Key point:
- Relevance and faithful representation are fundamental.
- Comparability, verifiability, timeliness and understandability enhance usefulness.
22. Q: What makes financial information relevant to users?
A: Show answer
Information is relevant when it is capable of making a difference to decisions made by users. It may have predictive value, confirmatory value or both. Predictive value means that the information can be used as an input to a user's model for estimating future outcomes; it does not mean that the information itself must be a forecast. Confirmatory value means that the information provides feedback that confirms or changes earlier expectations. Historical performance, for example, may help a lender assess future debt-servicing capacity and evaluate whether prior forecasts were realistic. Relevance depends on the decision context and the nature of the information.

Study instruction:
- "Take one historical financial measure and explain separately how it could have predictive value and confirmatory value."

Key point:
- Relevant information is capable of influencing a user's decision.
- Predictive and confirmatory value can exist in the same information.
23. Q: How do predictive value and confirmatory value differ?
A: Show answer
Predictive value exists when information can be used as an input into a user's process for estimating future outcomes. Confirmatory value exists when information provides feedback about previous assessments and helps users confirm or revise their expectations. For example, current operating margins may inform a forecast of future cash generation and also allow an investor to compare actual performance with an earlier forecast. The information does not need to have been used successfully in the past to possess predictive value; it must be capable of making a difference. Both qualities support the broader objective of decision-useful reporting.

Study instruction:
- "Use one financial metric to create a forward-looking analysis and a separate review of a previous expectation."

Key point:
- Predictive value informs expectations about the future.
- Confirmatory value provides feedback on past or present expectations.
24. Q: How should materiality be assessed under the Conceptual Framework?
A: Show answer
Materiality is an entity-specific aspect of relevance rather than a separate qualitative characteristic. Information is material when it is reasonable to expect that omitting, misstating or obscuring it could influence decisions made by primary users about the particular reporting entity. Size matters, but nature and context matter too. A monetary error that is small for a large group may be significant for a small entity, while a small payment involving fraud, a breach of law or senior management misconduct may be material because of its nature. Materiality therefore requires judgement about both quantitative magnitude and qualitative circumstances.

Study instruction:
- "Assess one item using both a quantitative benchmark and qualitative factors such as fraud, covenant sensitivity or management involvement."

Key point:
- Materiality is specific to the entity and the decisions of its primary users.
- Nature can make a small amount material even when its size is not significant.
25. Q: What does faithful representation require?
A: Show answer
Faithful representation requires a depiction of an economic phenomenon to be complete, neutral and free from material error. Completeness means including the information necessary for users to understand the phenomenon. Neutrality means the depiction is not selected or presented to produce a favourable or unfavourable response. Freedom from material error concerns the description and the process used to produce the information; it does not mean every estimate must be perfectly precise. The objective is to represent the economic substance of the transaction or event, not merely to reproduce legal wording or a preferred management narrative.

Study instruction:
- "Review a disclosure and test whether it is complete, neutral and free from material error in both description and preparation."

Key point:
- Faithful representation has three components: completeness, neutrality and freedom from material error.
- The economic substance must be represented rather than selectively described.
26. Q: How do neutrality and prudence work together in financial reporting?
A: Show answer
Neutrality means information is not biased toward a desired outcome. Prudence requires caution when making judgements under uncertainty, but it does not authorise deliberate understatement of assets or income or deliberate overstatement of liabilities or expenses. The chapter explains that prudence is used to support neutrality by discouraging optimistic or pessimistic bias. For example, an estimate should reflect the evidence and uncertainty rather than automatically selecting the lowest asset amount or highest liability amount. A cautious estimate can still be biased if it systematically favours one outcome. Preparers should document assumptions and apply them consistently with the underlying evidence.

Study instruction:
- "Review an uncertain estimate and identify whether caution has been applied neutrally or has become systematic bias."

Key point:
- Prudence is exercised in support of neutrality, not as permission for hidden reserves.
- Both optimistic and pessimistic bias undermine faithful representation.
27. Q: Does freedom from error mean that financial statement estimates must be exact?
A: Show answer
No. Many financial statement amounts cannot be observed directly and must be estimated. An estimate can still be faithfully represented when its nature is clearly described, the limitations of the estimation process are explained and the method has been selected and applied without material error. Freedom from error therefore concerns the quality of the description and the process, not a guarantee that an uncertain future outcome will match the estimate exactly. A preparer should disclose significant assumptions and measurement uncertainty where required, use appropriate evidence and avoid presenting an estimate as though it were an observable fact.

Study instruction:
- "Choose an estimate such as an impairment input or useful life and list the assumptions, evidence and limitations that should be documented."

Key point:
- An estimate is not automatically erroneous because it is uncertain.
- The method, assumptions and limitations must be described faithfully.
28. Q: How should relevance and faithful representation be balanced when they point to different measurements?
A: Show answer
The Framework treats relevance and faithful representation as complementary but recognises that a measurement offering the most relevant information may be difficult to represent faithfully. The decision process begins by identifying which economic information is relevant to users, then selecting a method that can depict that information faithfully. If a highly uncertain fair value cannot be represented with sufficient reliability, another measurement such as cost may provide a more faithful depiction while remaining useful. The choice is not a mechanical preference for cost or fair value; it requires judgement about the nature of the asset or liability, available evidence and the reporting objective.

Study instruction:
- "Analyse a case where fair value is more relevant but highly uncertain, and explain why a different measurement may be more useful overall."

Key point:
- Relevance identifies what information matters; faithful representation governs how it is depicted.
- A less directly relevant measurement may be preferable if the alternative cannot be represented faithfully.
29. Q: What is comparability and why does it matter to financial statement users?
A: Show answer
Comparability enables users to identify similarities and differences between economic phenomena. It supports analysis of trends within one entity across periods and comparisons between entities. Investors may use comparable margin or leverage information to evaluate performance, while lenders may compare liquidity and debt measures across borrowers. Comparability does not mean every entity must use identical accounting methods regardless of circumstances. Different methods may be appropriate for different transactions. The objective is to help users understand meaningful similarities and differences rather than create superficial uniformity that hides the underlying economics.

Study instruction:
- "Compare two periods or two entities and identify which accounting-policy or presentation differences affect the usefulness of the comparison."

Key point:
- Comparability helps users identify similarities and differences across entities and periods.
- Useful comparison requires transparent explanations of genuine differences.
30. Q: How does consistency differ from comparability?
A: Show answer
Consistency is the use of the same methods for the same items across periods or across entities in comparable circumstances. It supports comparability, but it is not the same thing. Comparability is the desired quality of information; consistency is one means of achieving it. Consistency does not require one method for every different class of asset or transaction. Nor should it prevent an entity from changing an accounting policy when a better method produces more relevant and faithfully represented information and the applicable Standard permits or requires the change. Blind uniformity can reduce, rather than improve, the quality of reporting.

Study instruction:
- "Identify a case where consistent treatment supports comparison and another where a justified policy change would improve information."

Key point:
- Consistency supports comparability but is not an end in itself.
- A better accounting method should not be blocked merely to preserve uniformity.
31. Q: What is verifiability and how can it be achieved?
A: Show answer
Verifiability gives users confidence that information faithfully represents the economic phenomena it purports to represent. It exists when knowledgeable and independent observers can reach a general consensus that a description or amount is a faithful representation, even if they do not agree on every detail. Direct verification may involve checking an invoice, bank confirmation or physical count. Indirect verification may involve checking the inputs and recalculating a model. Some forward-looking estimates cannot be fully verified, but they may still be useful. The chapter treats verifiability as highly desirable rather than an absolute condition for usefulness.

Study instruction:
- "For a reported amount, distinguish direct verification of the item from indirect verification of the model and its inputs."

Key point:
- Independent knowledgeable observers should be able to reach a general consensus.
- Lack of complete verifiability does not automatically make an estimate useless.
32. Q: Why is timeliness an enhancing qualitative characteristic?
A: Show answer
Timeliness means that information is available to users while it can still influence their decisions. Even accurate information may lose relevance if it arrives after investors, lenders or other users have made the decision for which it was needed. Timeliness can require efficient close processes, prompt review of estimates and disciplined publication schedules. However, speed should not be achieved by sacrificing completeness or faithful representation. Information may also remain useful after its initial publication when users analyse trends or compare results over time. The reporting process must therefore balance prompt delivery with adequate evidence and review.

Study instruction:
- "Map a reporting timetable and identify where delays could cause information to lose decision usefulness."

Key point:
- Information must arrive before it loses its ability to influence decisions.
- Faster reporting must not undermine accuracy and completeness.
33. Q: What does understandability require of financial reporting?
A: Show answer
Understandability requires information to be classified, characterised and presented clearly and concisely. The Framework assumes that users have reasonable knowledge of business and economic activities and will review information diligently; users may also seek advice to understand complex matters. This does not permit an entity to omit a complex transaction merely because it is difficult to explain. Instead, the preparer should organise the information, use clear terminology, distinguish material components and explain significant judgements. Effective communication makes complex information accessible without concealing its economic substance.

Study instruction:
- "Rewrite a technical note so that a financially literate reader can identify the transaction, judgement, amount and uncertainty without losing important detail."

Key point:
- Clarity and concise organisation improve understandability.
- Complex but relevant information should be explained, not automatically omitted.
34. Q: How does the cost constraint limit useful financial reporting?
A: Show answer
Providing information incurs costs, including collecting, processing, verifying and disseminating it. Users also incur costs when information is not provided and they must obtain it elsewhere. The Framework requires consideration of whether the benefits of information justify its costs. This is not a blanket exemption from a specific IFRS disclosure requirement; the applicable Standard governs mandatory information. The cost-benefit assessment is relevant to standard setting and to judgements permitted by the requirements. Preparers should consider whether a proposed disclosure or measurement process gives users meaningful benefits relative to the resources and complexity required.

Study instruction:
- "For a proposed disclosure, identify the users who benefit, the decisions it supports and the direct and indirect costs of producing it."

Key point:
- The benefits of reporting information should justify the costs of providing it.
- Cost considerations do not override mandatory requirements in an applicable Standard.
35. Q: What is the going concern assumption and why is it important?
A: Show answer
The going concern assumption means that financial statements are prepared on the basis that the entity will continue operating for the foreseeable future and long enough to carry out its existing commitments. It affects how assets and liabilities are understood and measured. For example, assets such as plant may be depreciated over their useful lives because they are expected to remain in use rather than be sold immediately. Prepayments and goodwill may also have value through continuing operations even when their immediate sale value is limited. Going concern is therefore a foundational assumption, not simply a statement that management hopes the business will survive.

Study instruction:
- "Identify three balances whose accounting interpretation depends on the entity continuing to operate rather than entering immediate liquidation."

Key point:
- Going concern assumes continued operation for the foreseeable future.
- The assumption affects the basis on which assets and liabilities are reported.
36. Q: When should an entity stop preparing financial statements on a going concern basis?
A: Show answer
The chapter states that the going concern assumption is set aside when management intends to liquidate the entity or cease trading, or has no realistic alternative to doing so. The financial statements must then be prepared on another basis. The Conceptual Framework does not prescribe that alternative basis; the chapter points to IAS 1 for disclosure requirements, including disclosure of the basis used when financial statements are not prepared on a going concern basis. Preparers must assess the facts, document management's plans and consider the applicable reporting requirements rather than continue using going concern assumptions automatically.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare an assessment checklist covering management intent, available alternatives, operating forecasts, financing and required disclosures."

Key point:
- Intent to liquidate or absence of a realistic alternative can invalidate going concern.
- The alternative basis and related disclosures must be determined under applicable requirements.
37. Q: How does the Conceptual Framework define an asset?
A: Show answer
An asset is a present economic resource controlled by the entity as a result of past events. The Framework defines an economic resource as a right that has the potential to produce economic benefits. The definition therefore focuses on the rights available to the entity, the entity's control over those rights and the existence of the rights at the reporting date. Physical possession or legal title alone does not settle the question. A preparer should identify the underlying right, establish how it arose, determine who controls it and assess whether it can potentially produce benefits for the reporting entity.

Study instruction:
- "Apply the three-part asset analysis to a piece of equipment, a prepaid service and a contractual right."

Key point:
- An asset is a present controlled economic resource arising from past events.
- The economic resource is a right with potential to produce benefits.
38. Q: Why does the asset definition focus on rights rather than only physical objects?
A: Show answer
A physical object may embody several rights, such as the right to use a machine and the right to obtain benefits from its output. The entity may account for those rights together as one asset when doing so provides a faithful and understandable depiction. Conversely, a single physical object may contain separable rights that need different analysis. The rights-based approach helps preparers focus on the economic resource rather than treating physical form as decisive. It also accommodates non-physical resources, such as contractual rights or prepaid services, that can provide economic benefits without a tangible object.

Study instruction:
- "For one physical asset, list the rights it contains and assess whether they should be treated together or separately for reporting."

Key point:
- The asset is the economic right or set of rights, not merely the physical object.
- A rights-based analysis also captures intangible and contractual resources.
39. Q: What does it mean for an economic resource to have the potential to produce economic benefits?
A: Show answer
A right has the potential to produce economic benefits when it can contribute to cash inflows, avoid cash outflows, be exchanged for another right on favourable terms, extinguish a liability or be used to create goods or services. A prepaid electricity balance, for example, represents a right to future electricity and can avoid a later cash payment. The potential does not need to be probable or likely to satisfy the asset definition. Probability and uncertainty are considered in recognition and measurement. This distinction prevents the definition stage from being confused with the later decision about whether and at what amount the item should be recognised.

Study instruction:
- "List the possible economic benefits of a contractual right and distinguish the existence of potential from the probability of receiving benefits."

Key point:
- Economic benefits include cash inflows, avoided outflows and productive use.
- Probability is considered in recognition or measurement rather than built into the asset definition.
40. Q: Does an entity need legal ownership to control an asset?
A: Show answer
No. The chapter explains that control can arise through the ability to enforce legal rights or through the ability to direct the use of a resource and obtain its benefits. Legal title is relevant evidence but is not always decisive. An entity may control a resource under a contract even when another party holds legal ownership, while legal ownership may not be enough if the entity cannot direct use or obtain benefits. The analysis should examine the substantive rights, restrictions, decision-making power and benefits available at the reporting date. This avoids classifying assets solely by reference to whose name appears on a title document.

Study instruction:
- "Compare legal title, contractual rights and the ability to direct use in a leasing or service arrangement."

Key point:
- Control is about enforceable rights or the ability to direct use and obtain benefits.
- Legal ownership is not an automatic requirement or a complete test of control.
41. Q: Why must an asset arise from a past event?
A: Show answer
The asset definition requires a present right at the reporting date that resulted from an event occurring on or before that date. A plan to acquire equipment next month does not create a present asset today because the entity does not yet hold the relevant right. By contrast, once a purchase, contract or other event gives the entity a present controlled right, the asset definition may be satisfied even if the benefits will be received later. The past-event requirement establishes a reporting-date boundary and prevents management intentions or future plans from being recorded as existing resources.

Study instruction:
- "For a planned purchase, signed contract and delivered asset, identify the date on which the entity obtains the relevant present right."

Key point:
- An asset must exist at the reporting date because of a past event.
- Future intentions alone do not create a present economic resource.
42. Q: How does the Conceptual Framework define a liability?
A: Show answer
A liability is a present obligation of the entity to transfer an economic resource as a result of past events. A present obligation is a duty or responsibility that the entity has no practical ability to avoid. The analysis therefore asks whether an obligation exists now, whether the entity may have to transfer an economic resource and whether the obligation arose from a past event. The resource transferred need not be cash; it can include goods, services, another asset or a right to cash. The definition focuses on the substance of the obligation rather than the label used in a contract or management report.

Study instruction:
- "Apply the present-obligation, transfer-of-resource and past-event tests to a supplier balance and a proposed future purchase."

Key point:
- A liability is a present unavoidable obligation arising from past events.
- The transfer can involve cash or another economic resource.
43. Q: What does it mean that an entity has no practical ability to avoid a present obligation?
A: Show answer
An entity has no practical ability to avoid an obligation when the duty or responsibility cannot realistically be avoided through its ordinary decisions. The chapter notes that an entity cannot treat cessation of trading as a practical escape route when it is assumed to continue as a going concern. Obligations are owed to a third party, although the third party need not always be identified by name. The obligation may be legal, constructive or conditional. Preparers should examine the entity's actual practices, public statements, contractual terms and circumstances rather than assume that an obligation exists only when a formal invoice or court order has arrived.

Study instruction:
- "For a disputed obligation, identify the third party, the source of the duty and whether the entity has a realistic way to avoid it."

Key point:
- A present obligation is a duty the entity cannot practically avoid.
- The obligation involves a third party even if that party is not individually identified.
44. Q: How do legal, constructive and conditional obligations differ?
A: Show answer
A legal obligation is enforceable through law or a contract. A constructive obligation arises from an entity's customary practices, published policies or specific statements that create valid expectations among other parties. A conditional obligation depends on future actions or events, but those actions may not be practically avoidable in the circumstances. The chapter illustrates this with a mining company whose licence and operating activity can create a duty to rehabilitate land if specified damage occurs. The classification helps preparers understand why a liability may exist even without a conventional invoice, while still requiring careful analysis of the facts and the applicable Standard.

Study instruction:
- "Classify three examples as legal, constructive or conditional and identify the evidence that supports each conclusion."

Key point:
- Legal obligations arise from enforceable requirements; constructive obligations arise from established expectations.
- Conditional obligations can arise from future actions that the entity cannot practically avoid.
45. Q: How is a future commitment different from a present liability?
A: Show answer
A future commitment or intention to sacrifice resources does not ordinarily create a present liability if the entity can still avoid the future outflow. A management decision to buy equipment next year is therefore not enough by itself. A present obligation may arise when the entity enters an irrevocable agreement or takes an action that creates an unavoidable duty, such as a binding purchase contract with a substantial cancellation penalty. The distinction depends on the rights and obligations existing at the reporting date, not on how strongly management expects the transaction to occur. This prevents planned expenditure from being recorded prematurely as a liability.

Study instruction:
- "Compare an approved purchase request, a cancellable order and an irrevocable contract and identify when an obligation becomes present."

Key point:
- Intentions and future plans are not automatically present obligations.
- An unavoidable duty created by a past event may meet the liability definition.
46. Q: What is the past-event test for a liability?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains the liability past-event test through a cause-and-effect relationship. The entity must have obtained a benefit or taken an action, and as a result it may have to transfer an economic resource. Work already performed by employees is an example: the entity has received labour and must pay wages, so a present obligation exists. Wages for work that employees will perform next month are different because the service has not yet been received and the relevant past event has not occurred. Preparers should identify the event that created the obligation and link it to the required transfer.

Study instruction:
- "For wages, supplier services and a future service contract, identify the benefit or action already received and the resulting obligation."

Key point:
- A liability requires a past event that creates a present duty to transfer resources.
- Payment for future work is not the same as an obligation for work already performed.
47. Q: How does the Conceptual Framework define income?
A: Show answer
Income consists of increases in assets or decreases in liabilities, except changes arising from contributions by holders of equity claims. The definition links income directly to the other elements of the statement of financial position. Cash received from a shareholder in exchange for shares increases assets, but it is not income because it is an owner contribution. A reduction in a loan liability may create income when the creditor forgives the debt, unless the forgiveness is effectively an owner contribution. The preparer must therefore identify the source and substance of the change rather than classify every increase in cash or decrease in debt as income.

Study instruction:
- "Analyse a cash receipt, debt forgiveness and shareholder contribution and explain which changes create income and which change equity directly."

Key point:
- Income arises from increases in assets or decreases in liabilities, subject to the owner-contribution exception.
- The source of the change determines whether it is income or an equity movement.
48. Q: What is the difference between income, revenue and gains?
A: Show answer
Income is the broad Framework element. The chapter explains revenue as income arising in the course of an entity's ordinary activities, while gains are income arising from other activities or events, such as selling a non-current asset outside the entity's ordinary operations. Revenue is generally presented gross, whereas gains are often presented net of related expenses, such as the carrying amount of equipment sold. The chapter cites the former IAS 18 wording as an illustration of revenue; current recognition and measurement must be determined under the applicable IFRS Standard, particularly where later Standards have replaced earlier requirements. The conceptual distinction should not be used as a substitute for a current revenue analysis.

Study instruction:
- "Classify sales, interest, a disposal gain and an owner contribution, then identify which current Standard governs recognition."

Key point:
- Revenue and gains are both forms of income but arise from different activities.
- The applicable current Standard, not a historical definition alone, governs recognition.
49. Q: How does the Conceptual Framework define expenses?
A: Show answer
Expenses are decreases in assets or increases in liabilities, other than distributions to holders of equity claims. The definition mirrors the income definition and links expenses to movements in assets and liabilities. Paying for electricity already consumed reduces cash without creating a future resource, so it produces an expense. Paying in advance for electricity not yet used may exchange one asset, cash, for another asset, a right to future electricity, and therefore does not create an immediate expense merely because cash has been paid. A dividend paid to shareholders is a distribution of equity, not an expense. The substance and timing of the transaction matter.

Study instruction:
- "Compare an advance payment, consumption of a service and a dividend and trace the effect on assets, liabilities, expenses and equity."

Key point:
- Expenses arise from asset decreases or liability increases, subject to owner distributions.
- A cash payment is not automatically an expense if it creates another asset.
50. Q: What is equity under the Conceptual Framework?
A: Show answer
Equity is the residual interest in the assets of the entity after deducting all liabilities. It is not defined independently of assets and liabilities. Equity increases through profitable operations and contributions from owners, and decreases through losses and distributions to owners. It can be divided into components such as contributed capital, retained earnings and reserves, depending on the entity's legal and reporting structure. Because equity is residual, changes in measurement of assets and liabilities affect its amount. The capital concept and capital-maintenance basis used by an entity can also influence how profit and equity movements are understood.

Study instruction:
- "Reconcile opening equity to closing equity by separating profit or loss, owner contributions, owner distributions and other recognised movements."

Key point:
- Equity is the residual after liabilities are deducted from assets.
- Profit, losses, owner contributions and distributions explain major equity movements.
51. Q: What conditions must be met before an item is recognised in the financial statements?
A: Show answer
An item must first meet the definition of one of the five elements: asset, liability, equity, income or expense. It must then meet the recognition criteria in the 2018 Conceptual Framework. Recognition should provide relevant information and a faithful representation of the item. These two criteria apply across the five elements, replacing the older approach that used different recognition tests for different elements. Meeting a definition is therefore necessary but not sufficient. The preparer must also assess whether recognising the item will produce useful information, considering uncertainty, measurement and the circumstances of the transaction.

Study instruction:
- "For a proposed asset or liability, document separately how it meets the element definition and how recognition would provide relevant, faithfully represented information."

Key point:
- Recognition requires both an element definition and useful information.
- The two fundamental qualitative characteristics form the recognition criteria.
52. Q: How do existence, outcome and measurement uncertainty affect recognition?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies three uncertainties that affect recognition decisions. Existence uncertainty concerns whether an asset or liability exists. Outcome uncertainty concerns the amount and timing of potential economic benefits or transfers. Measurement uncertainty arises when the amount cannot be observed directly and must be estimated. Existence and outcome uncertainty can affect relevance: if the item is highly unlikely to exist or its possible effect is clearly insignificant, recognition may not provide useful information. Measurement uncertainty affects faithful representation: an estimate may still be useful if the method is appropriate and its limitations are explained, but uncertainty can become so high that another measurement basis is needed.

Study instruction:
- "For a legal claim, possible rehabilitation duty and fair-value estimate, identify which uncertainty is present and which recognition quality it affects."

Key point:
- Existence and outcome uncertainty primarily affect relevance.
- Measurement uncertainty primarily affects faithful representation.
53. Q: What should an entity do when an item meets a definition but fails the recognition criteria?
A: Show answer
An item that fails the definition or recognition criteria is not recognised in the statement of financial position or statement of profit or loss and other comprehensive income. However, information about the item may still be useful to users and may need to be disclosed in the notes, subject to the applicable Standard. An item not recognised in the current period may become recognisable later if the facts change or the recognition criteria are then met. Preparers should preserve evidence and reassess material changes rather than treating non-recognition as a permanent conclusion. Disclosure does not convert an unrecognised item into a recognised balance.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a note-disclosure assessment for a material uncertain item that is not recognised and identify what future event would trigger reassessment."

Key point:
- Failure of recognition does not necessarily mean the information is irrelevant to users.
- A previously unrecognised item may be recognised later when the criteria are met.
54. Q: What is derecognition and when does it occur?
A: Show answer
Derecognition is the removal of all or part of a recognised asset or liability from the statement of financial position. It normally occurs when the item, or the relevant portion, no longer meets its definition. An asset may be derecognised when the entity loses control of the right; a liability may be derecognised when the entity no longer has a present obligation for all or part of it. The analysis is not limited to cash settlement. A transfer, expiry, cancellation or other event may change the rights and obligations. Preparers must identify the precise portion affected and ensure that the remaining balance is still faithfully represented.

Study instruction:
- "For a partial asset disposal or partial release of an obligation, identify the portion derecognised and the balance that remains."

Key point:
- Derecognition removes all or part of a recognised asset or liability.
- Loss of control or release from an obligation can trigger derecognition.
55. Q: What does measurement mean in the Conceptual Framework?
A: Show answer
Measurement is the quantification of recognised elements in monetary terms. It requires selecting a measurement basis for assets and liabilities, with related effects on income, expenses and equity. The Framework groups measurement bases into historical cost and current value. It explains factors that standard setters consider when designing requirements and can help preparers when an applicable IFRS Standard allows a choice. In most cases, preparers must follow the measurement basis specified by the relevant Standard rather than independently select whichever basis they prefer. Measurement should produce information that is relevant, faithfully represented and useful relative to its cost.

Study instruction:
- "Identify the measurement basis required or permitted for three balances and explain how each basis affects the reported amount."

Key point:
- Measurement assigns monetary amounts to recognised elements.
- The applicable Standard determines which measurement bases are permitted or required.
56. Q: What is historical cost and how is it applied?
A: Show answer
Historical cost is based on the price of the transaction or other event that created the asset or liability. It normally includes transaction costs and may subsequently be adjusted for consumption, depreciation, amortisation or impairment. The chapter gives examples including property, plant and equipment, inventory measured using cost subject to the relevant lower-of-cost requirement, and certain financial liabilities measured at amortised cost. Historical cost is an entry-price basis because it reflects the acquisition transaction. It can provide a verifiable starting point, but it may become less relevant when prices or economic conditions change substantially after acquisition.

Study instruction:
- "Trace an asset from initial transaction price through transaction costs, depreciation or amortisation and impairment adjustments."

Key point:
- Historical cost begins with the price of the originating transaction.
- Subsequent adjustments may reflect use and impairment under the applicable Standard.
57. Q: What measurement bases are included within current value?
A: Show answer
The Framework describes current value as reflecting conditions at the measurement date. The chapter identifies fair value, current cost, value in use for an asset and fulfilment value for a liability. Fair value is an exit price in an orderly transaction between market participants. Current cost is an entry price for an equivalent asset in its current condition or an equivalent liability. Value in use reflects the present value of benefits the entity expects from using and eventually disposing of an asset. Fulfilment value reflects the present value of the resources the entity expects to transfer to settle a liability. These bases answer different measurement questions and should not be treated as interchangeable.

Study instruction:
- "Create a comparison table showing whether each current-value basis is an entry or exit perspective and whose assumptions it reflects."

Key point:
- Current value reflects conditions at the measurement date.
- Fair value, current cost, value in use and fulfilment value have distinct measurement objectives.
58. Q: How does fair value differ from current cost?
A: Show answer
Fair value is an exit-price measurement: the price that would be received to sell an asset or paid to transfer a liability in an orderly transaction between market participants at the measurement date. Current cost is an entry-price measurement: the consideration required to acquire an equivalent asset in its current condition, or the consideration received for an equivalent liability. The chapter illustrates current cost with a machine originally bought new for $1,000 that would cost $900 to replace in its present three-year-old condition; the current cost is $900, not the original price or the cost of a new machine. The distinction is between market-participant exit value and replacement-based entry value.

Study instruction:
- "Work through a replacement-cost example and compare the current-condition amount with a market exit price."

Key point:
- Fair value is an exit price; current cost is an entry price.
- Current cost reflects an equivalent item in its present condition.
59. Q: What are value in use and fulfilment value?
A: Show answer
Value in use is the present value of the economic benefits an entity expects to obtain from using an asset and ultimately disposing of it. It reflects the entity's expected use of the asset and is used in impairment analysis under relevant requirements. Fulfilment value is the present value of the economic resources the entity expects to transfer in fulfilling a liability. One focuses on benefits from an asset; the other focuses on outflows required to settle an obligation. Both involve estimates of future cash flows and discounting, so assumptions, uncertainty and the applicable Standard's detailed rules must be considered.

Study instruction:
- "Distinguish an asset-use cash-flow model from a liability-settlement cash-flow model and identify the assumptions each requires."

Key point:
- Value in use measures expected benefits from using and disposing of an asset.
- Fulfilment value measures expected resources transferred to settle a liability.
60. Q: How should an entity choose an appropriate measurement basis?
A: Show answer
The choice should produce information that is relevant and faithfully represented, while also considering comparability, verifiability, timeliness, understandability and cost. The nature of the asset or liability and how users will use the information matter. A directly observable price may be faithfully represented but not relevant if the asset is used internally and will not be sold. Conversely, an estimate may be relevant but too uncertain to be represented faithfully. The Framework encourages preparers and standard setters to consider both initial and subsequent measurement, and to avoid choices that create misleading gains or losses merely because the basis changes without an underlying transaction.

Study instruction:
- "For an asset with both an observable market price and continuing internal use, assess which measurement better serves users and can be represented faithfully."

Key point:
- Measurement choices should balance relevance, faithful representation and cost.
- A directly observable amount is not automatically the most relevant amount.
61. Q: How can measurement uncertainty and accounting mismatches affect usefulness?
A: Show answer
Measurement uncertainty arises when an amount must be estimated rather than directly observed. It does not automatically invalidate the measurement, but a high degree of uncertainty may make the information unfaithful and require another basis or clearer disclosure. The Framework also warns about accounting mismatches when related assets and liabilities use different measurement bases. If cash flows from an asset are linked to a liability but the two are measured inconsistently, reported income or equity movements may fail to depict the relationship faithfully. Preparers should consider the combined effects of related items and the applicable Standard's requirements rather than assessing each balance in isolation.

Study instruction:
- "Identify a linked asset and liability and explain how different measurement bases could create volatility or obscure their economic relationship."

Key point:
- Measurement uncertainty must be assessed for its effect on faithful representation.
- Different bases for related items can create accounting mismatches.
62. Q: What is a unit of account and why is it important?
A: Show answer
A unit of account is the right or group of rights, obligation or group of obligations, or group of rights and obligations to which recognition criteria and measurement concepts are applied. Choosing the unit determines what is assessed together and what is assessed separately. An entity might assess each contract individually for recognition but measure recognised contracts as a portfolio when that produces useful information and is cost-effective. The appropriate unit should reflect the substance of the transaction and the objective of providing relevant, faithfully represented information. A poor choice can conceal distinct rights or obligations or create unnecessary complexity.

Study instruction:
- "For a portfolio of similar contracts, explain whether recognition and measurement should use the same unit of account and why."

Key point:
- The unit of account defines the rights or obligations assessed for recognition and measurement.
- Different units may be appropriate for recognition and measurement when justified.
63. Q: What is the difference between presentation and disclosure?
A: Show answer
Presentation concerns how recognised elements are organised and shown in the primary financial statements, such as the statement of financial position and statement of profit or loss and other comprehensive income. Disclosure provides additional information, often in the notes, about recognised items and sometimes about matters not recognised because definitions or recognition criteria are not met. Both are communication decisions and should help users understand the entity's financial position, performance and cash flows. The chapter emphasises classifying similar items together, separating dissimilar items and avoiding both excessive detail and excessive aggregation that obscures important information.

Study instruction:
- "Take one recognised balance and identify what belongs on the face of the statement and what additional explanation belongs in the notes."

Key point:
- Presentation organises information in the primary statements.
- Disclosure supplies explanatory detail that helps users understand recognised and unrecognised matters.
64. Q: What is capital maintenance and how does it affect the recognition of profit?
A: Show answer
Capital maintenance is the concept used to determine when an entity has preserved the capital it seeks to maintain. Profit is recognised only to the extent that closing capital exceeds the capital existing at the beginning of the period after adjusting for contributions from and distributions to owners. The concept prevents the entity from treating a mere recovery of the capital base as profit. The Framework distinguishes financial capital from physical capital, and the choice affects what must be maintained before profit can be said to arise. The user needs of the financial statements guide the choice of capital concept.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a simple opening-to-closing capital reconciliation and adjust for owner contributions and distributions before identifying profit."

Key point:
- Profit is recognised only after the relevant capital has been maintained.
- Owner contributions and distributions must be separated from operating performance.
65. Q: How do financial capital and physical capital concepts differ?
A: Show answer
Under the financial capital concept, capital is the entity's net assets or equity, measured in nominal monetary units or units of constant purchasing power. Profit exists when closing financial capital exceeds opening capital after owner contributions and distributions are adjusted. Under the physical capital concept, capital represents the entity's operating capability. Profit arises only when that capability is maintained or increased after the same owner-related adjustments. The chapter notes that most entities use the financial capital concept, while a physical-capital approach would require current-cost measurement. The choice should reflect users' information needs and the purpose of the financial statements.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a financial-capital calculation with a physical-capital example in which replacement capacity must be preserved."

Key point:
- Financial capital focuses on net assets or equity.
- Physical capital focuses on operating capability and is associated with current-cost measurement.
66. Q: What does academic research on value relevance seek to establish about financial statements?
A: Show answer
Value-relevance research examines whether accounting amounts and disclosures are associated with market measures such as share prices, share returns or trading activity. The chapter describes early evidence that earnings surprises and stock returns move in related directions, and that trading volume increases around earnings announcements, suggesting that investors respond to reported information. Such evidence supports the proposition that accounting numbers can provide decision-useful information. It does not by itself prove that accounting information caused every market movement, because prices also reflect other news and expectations. Researchers therefore distinguish association, predictive usefulness and causal claims when interpreting empirical results.

Study instruction:
- "Explain what a positive association between earnings surprises and share returns can show and identify what it cannot prove about causation."

Key point:
- Value-relevance research tests whether accounting information is associated with market decisions and outcomes.
- Association is evidence of information usefulness, not automatic proof of causation.
Rows: 66
Table 2
Clarity and Precision in ChatGPT Prompts
1. Q: What does clarity and precision mean when writing a prompt for ChatGPT?
A: Show answer
Clarity means expressing the request so that its purpose and intended meaning are easy to understand. Precision means supplying enough relevant detail to identify exactly what should be addressed and what kind of result is wanted. A clear prompt avoids unnecessary ambiguity; a precise prompt narrows the task, subject, scope, conditions, and expected presentation. For example, asking for information about dogs is understandable at a broad level but leaves the breed, purpose, and depth open. Asking for an overview of the dietary needs and common health issues of Labrador Retrievers gives the system a more defined task. Clarity and precision therefore work together: the request should be understandable and sufficiently bounded to guide a relevant answer.

Study instruction:
- "Rewrite one broad prompt twice: first to make its meaning unambiguous, then to add the details needed to make the requested result specific."

Key point:
- Clarity makes the intended request understandable; precision defines the required subject and result.
- A useful prompt gives enough direction to reduce avoidable assumptions.
2. Q: Why does prompt clarity have such a strong effect on the usefulness of a ChatGPT response?
A: Show answer
A prompt is the main description of the task the model must perform. When the request is clear, the model has a better basis for selecting relevant information and organizing a response around the user's objective. When the request is unclear, the model must infer what the user probably means, and those inferences may not match the user's actual need. This can produce material that is technically related to the words in the prompt but too general, incomplete, or focused on the wrong aspect. Clear instructions also reduce the need for repeated clarification and correction. The chapter's central point is that the quality of the interaction depends substantially on how clearly the request is communicated.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a response to a vague request with one to a clarified request and identify which differences came from the prompt rather than the subject itself."

Key point:
- The prompt supplies the direction for the response.
- Clear direction reduces irrelevant assumptions and avoidable rework.
3. Q: How does using direct language improve a prompt?
A: Show answer
Direct language states the requested action plainly instead of making the model infer what the user wants. A prompt such as Explain the causes of the delay is more direct than a fragment such as Something about the delay. Direct wording normally identifies an action such as explain, compare, summarize, calculate, classify, or draft. It should also name the subject of that action. Direct language does not require an abrupt tone or an unnecessarily short prompt; it requires a clear statement of the task. Additional context can then be added after the core request so that the model can distinguish the main action from supporting details.

Study instruction:
- "Take three incomplete or indirect requests and rewrite each as a direct instruction beginning with a precise action verb."

Key point:
- State the action the model should perform.
- Direct wording can still include all necessary context and constraints.
4. Q: What kinds of ambiguity should be removed from a prompt?
A: Show answer
A prompt may be ambiguous about the subject, the meaning of a key term, the intended audience, the time period, the geographic area, the level of detail, or the required output. Words such as recent, best, simple, comprehensive, and appropriate can have different meanings depending on the context. Pronouns such as it, they, or this may also be unclear when several possible referents appear in the request. Ambiguity is reduced by naming the relevant object, defining terms that could be interpreted differently, and specifying any context that changes the answer. The goal is not to eliminate every possible interpretation, but to remove uncertainties that could materially change the result.

Study instruction:
- "Review a prompt and underline words or references that could reasonably be interpreted in more than one way; replace them with specific terms."

Key point:
- Ambiguity can concern the subject, context, scope, terminology, or format.
- Clarify uncertainties that could change the substance of the answer.
5. Q: Which details should be included to make a prompt more specific?
A: Show answer
Include details that materially affect the answer: the subject, relevant background, intended purpose, audience, scope, constraints, and desired format. For a practical task, the user's starting point and desired outcome may also matter. A request for a cake recipe becomes more useful when it identifies the type of cake, dietary requirement, number of servings, and any relevant equipment limitation. A request for an analysis becomes more useful when it identifies the decision being supported and the information that should be considered. Details that do not influence the task can usually be omitted. Specificity is not the same as adding as many words as possible; it is selecting the information that helps define the work.
