Study instruction:
- "For one prompt, list the details that would change the answer and separate them from background information that would not affect the result."

Key point:
- Add details that materially guide the requested response.
- Relevant specificity is more valuable than unnecessary length.
6. Q: How can a user decide whether a piece of context is relevant to a prompt?
A: Show answer
Context is relevant when it helps determine what answer would satisfy the request. Ask whether the information changes the subject, the assumptions, the applicable conditions, the audience, the level of detail, or the format. For example, a request for a project plan may need the project objective, deadline, team size, and known constraints because these facts affect the plan. A personal anecdote unrelated to the project may add length without improving the result. It can be useful to state why a context detail matters, especially when its importance is not obvious. If removing a detail would not change the expected answer, that detail may not need to be included.

Study instruction:
- "Use a real task and test each contextual detail by asking whether removing it would materially change the expected answer."

Key point:
- Context should help the model interpret or complete the task.
- Unrelated background can obscure the main request.
7. Q: What does defining the scope of a prompt involve?
A: Show answer
Defining scope means setting the boundaries of the subject and work requested. It tells the model which aspects to cover and, where useful, which aspects to leave out. A prompt about project management could be limited to risk identification and mitigation rather than asking for every project management discipline. Scope may be defined by topic, stage, population, time period, jurisdiction, department, or level of analysis. A well-bounded request helps prevent a response that is either too broad to be useful or so narrow that it misses an essential issue. The scope should be consistent with the user's objective and should not exclude information necessary to answer the question accurately.

Study instruction:
- "Take a broad topic and define its scope by naming the included dimensions and any important exclusions."

Key point:
- Scope establishes what the response should and should not cover.
- A suitable boundary improves relevance without removing necessary context.
8. Q: How are scope and constraints different in a prompt?
A: Show answer
Scope identifies the subject matter or parts of a task that should be addressed. Constraints identify conditions the response must respect while completing that task. For example, a scope might request a comparison of three project options, while constraints might require the comparison to use only supplied data, stay within a specified budget, and fit on one page. Scope answers the question What is included? Constraints answer the question Under what conditions must the work be done? Both are useful because a model can understand the general subject yet still produce an unsuitable result if important conditions are omitted. State each separately when the distinction matters.

Study instruction:
- "Write a prompt with one sentence defining its scope and a separate sentence stating two constraints."

Key point:
- Scope defines the coverage of the task.
- Constraints govern how the task must be completed.
9. Q: Why should a prompt state important conditions or constraints explicitly?
A: Show answer
A condition can change which answer is appropriate. If a user needs a recipe without gluten, a plan that includes ordinary wheat flour does not meet the request even if the rest of the recipe is sound. If an analysis must use only a supplied dataset, introducing unsupported outside figures would violate the task. Stating conditions makes these requirements visible instead of leaving the model to guess them. Useful constraints may concern sources, length, language, audience, budget, available resources, exclusions, or compliance with a specified procedure. Constraints should be realistic, compatible with one another, and specific enough to evaluate.

Study instruction:
- "Identify the non-negotiable conditions for a task and express each as a short, testable instruction."

Key point:
- Explicit constraints prevent responses that are relevant but unusable.
- A constraint should be clear enough to check against the finished answer.
10. Q: How should a user choose and specify the desired output format?
A: Show answer
Choose a format that supports the way the result will be used. A list may help when the user needs separate actions, a table may help compare several items, numbered steps may help explain a procedure, and a short summary may help with rapid review. The prompt should name the format directly and, when necessary, describe its structure. For example, request a table with columns for option, advantage, limitation, and risk rather than merely saying make it organized. Format instructions should not conflict with the task or make the answer difficult to read. If the format is flexible, the user can ask the model to choose the clearest presentation.

Study instruction:
- "Choose an output format for a comparison, a procedure, and an executive summary, and explain why each format fits its use."

Key point:
- Name the format when presentation affects usability.
- Describe the required structure when a format label alone is insufficient.
11. Q: What is the practical difference between a specific prompt and a vague prompt?
A: Show answer
A specific prompt narrows the task through clear wording and relevant details. A vague prompt leaves important choices unresolved, so the model must decide what subject, level, or direction is probably intended. The chapter illustrates this with a broad request to tell the user about dogs and a more specific request about the dietary needs and common health issues of Labrador Retrievers. The second prompt directs attention to a defined breed and two defined areas. Specific prompts are more likely to produce focused responses because they reduce the number of plausible interpretations. Vague prompts can still be useful for brainstorming, but they are less suitable when the user needs a particular answer.

Study instruction:
- "Create a vague and a specific version of the same request, then compare how the subject, boundaries, and expected answer change."

Key point:
- Specific language narrows the intended task.
- Vague language leaves more decisions to inference.
12. Q: Why do vague prompts often produce generic or irrelevant answers?
A: Show answer
A vague prompt provides too little direction to distinguish among the many possible responses that could fit its wording. The model may choose a broad overview because the user has not identified a particular angle, audience, or outcome. It may also devote space to topics the user did not intend to explore. For example, Tell me about dogs could produce a general description of dogs rather than information about nutrition, training, breed characteristics, or health. The answer may be coherent while still failing the user's unstated objective. The remedy is to specify the aspect of interest and the kind of information needed.

Study instruction:
- "When a response feels generic, identify the missing subject detail or intended outcome and add it to the original prompt."

Key point:
- A broad request invites a broad response.
- Specify the angle of interest to reduce irrelevant coverage.
13. Q: What assumptions may ChatGPT need to make when a prompt is underspecified?
A: Show answer
When a request omits important details, the model may have to infer the intended audience, meaning of terms, level of detail, context, and presentation. Those assumptions may be reasonable but still differ from what the user had in mind. A prompt asking for the best approach does not identify the criteria for best, so the response may prioritize cost when the user values reliability or speed. A prompt asking for a simple explanation does not define whether the audience is a child, a new employee, or a technical specialist. A stronger prompt supplies the criteria or asks for alternatives where the criteria are uncertain. Users should review assumptions that could materially affect decisions.

Study instruction:
- "Find three hidden assumptions in an underspecified request and rewrite it so that each important assumption is stated or made explicit as an open question."

Key point:
- Missing details force the model to infer user intent.
- State decision criteria when different assumptions would produce different recommendations.
14. Q: How does the chapter's dog example demonstrate prompt specificity?
A: Show answer
The broad request Tell me about dogs does not identify what the user wants to learn. Dogs could be discussed through breed history, behavior, care, training, nutrition, health, or many other subjects. The more specific version asks for an overview of the dietary needs and common health issues of Labrador Retrievers. It narrows the animal to a particular breed and identifies two areas of interest. This gives the response a clear direction while leaving room for a useful overview within those boundaries. The example demonstrates that adding a few carefully chosen details can improve relevance more effectively than simply making a prompt longer.

Study instruction:
- "Use the dog example as a model: specify the subject category, the particular subject, and the two or three aspects that matter."

Key point:
- A defined breed and defined topics make the request more focused.
- A small number of relevant details can substantially improve direction.
15. Q: How does the chapter's cake example show the value of adding practical conditions?
A: Show answer
The broad question How do I make a cake? leaves the type of cake, ingredients, dietary requirements, and serving needs open. The specific version requests the steps and ingredients for a gluten-free chocolate cake for eight people. It identifies the desired product, a dietary condition, the scale of the recipe, and the kinds of information required. These details change the practical answer: the ingredient choices and quantities must suit the requested cake and number of servings. The example shows that a prompt should include conditions that affect whether the result can actually be used, not merely the general subject.

Study instruction:
- "Rewrite a general how-to request by adding the intended result, relevant restrictions, scale, and requested components."

Key point:
- Practical conditions can change the content of an answer.
- State dietary, quantity, resource, or other task-specific requirements explicitly.
16. Q: How does the World War II example demonstrate the importance of narrowing a broad topic?
A: Show answer
The phrase Something about World War II gives almost no indication of the question the user wants answered. The specific version asks for the major causes of World War II and how they contributed to the start of the war in 1939. It defines the subject as causation, asks for major rather than incidental factors, and identifies the relationship that should be explained. This changes the task from a general description of a vast historical period into a focused causal explanation. The example illustrates that a prompt should identify the intellectual operation required, such as explaining causes, comparing outcomes, or tracing a sequence.

Study instruction:
- "Take a broad academic topic and specify whether the task is to explain causes, compare interpretations, evaluate evidence, or describe consequences."

Key point:
- A focused analytical question is more useful than an open-ended topic label.
- Name the relationship or type of reasoning the answer should explain.
17. Q: What repeatable method can transform a vague prompt into a specific one?
A: Show answer
Begin by identifying the task verb: explain, compare, summarize, recommend, plan, or analyze. Next, name the exact subject and the aspect that matters. Add relevant context such as the intended audience, purpose, starting conditions, or decision to be supported. Define the scope and state constraints that could change the answer. Finally, specify the desired output format and check whether any wording remains ambiguous. The chapter's examples follow this general pattern by moving from a broad subject to a defined subject and requested information. The method should be applied selectively; not every prompt needs every possible component.

Study instruction:
- "Apply the sequence task, subject, relevant context, scope, constraints, and format to one real request."

Key point:
- A structured rewrite turns an open topic into an actionable request.
- Include only the components that materially affect the task.
18. Q: Why should the main task be stated before adding background information?
A: Show answer
Stating the main task first helps establish what the prompt is asking the model to do. Background information can then be interpreted as context for that task rather than as a competing subject. For example, a user can begin with Compare two project options, then provide the project objective, budget, deadline, and decision criteria. If a prompt begins with a long history and never clearly states the requested action, the model may not know whether to summarize the background, assess it, or recommend a decision. A clear task statement provides an organizing point for the rest of the instructions.

Study instruction:
- "Reorder a long prompt so that its main requested action appears first and supporting context follows it."

Key point:
- The task statement tells the model what work to perform.
- Background is most useful when its role in the task is clear.
19. Q: How can a prompt identify its subject precisely?
A: Show answer
Name the specific object, group, process, document, or issue that the answer should address. Avoid relying on broad labels when a more exact name is available. Instead of asking about accounting, identify the transaction, reporting issue, or control being considered. Instead of asking about artificial intelligence generally, identify the system or application and the question being examined. If the subject has several possible versions, specify the edition, jurisdiction, model, date range, or other identifier that matters. Precise subject identification reduces the risk that the answer will discuss a related but different topic.

Study instruction:
- "Replace general subject labels in three prompts with exact names, versions, processes, or categories."

Key point:
- The subject should be identifiable without guessing.
- Use identifiers such as version, period, or jurisdiction when they affect the answer.
20. Q: Why is it useful to identify the intended audience in a prompt?
A: Show answer
The intended audience affects terminology, assumed background knowledge, examples, and level of explanation. A technical explanation for an experienced analyst may be unsuitable for a new employee who needs definitions and practical illustrations. A briefing for senior management may need implications and decisions rather than a detailed tutorial. Naming the audience helps the model choose how to communicate without changing the underlying facts. The user should describe the audience in functional terms, such as first-year students, nontechnical managers, or experienced practitioners. If the audience is mixed, state that the response should explain specialist terms while preserving necessary technical accuracy.

Study instruction:
- "Rewrite one topic prompt for a beginner and for an experienced practitioner, then identify the audience-related changes."

Key point:
- Audience information guides vocabulary, depth, and examples.
- Adjust presentation without compromising accuracy.
21. Q: How should a prompt specify the desired level of detail?
A: Show answer
Use a description that can be interpreted in relation to the task. Terms such as brief, introductory, detailed, step-by-step, or advanced are useful when paired with the expected purpose. For example, a concise executive summary differs from a detailed implementation guide even when both cover the same subject. The user can specify the number of major points, whether examples are needed, or which stages require explanation. Avoid relying only on vague adjectives such as comprehensive if the required coverage can be named more clearly. The desired depth should be proportionate to the task and the audience.

Study instruction:
- "For one subject, write a brief version, a practical step-by-step version, and an advanced analytical version of the prompt."

Key point:
- Describe detail in terms of the intended use and required coverage.
- Specific depth instructions are clearer than adjectives alone.
22. Q: How can a prompt make its requested format unambiguous?
A: Show answer
Name the format and describe the parts it should contain. A request for a table can specify its columns and the type of information in each column. A request for steps can specify that actions should be numbered in execution order. A request for a summary can specify the audience, approximate length, and topics to preserve. These details prevent the model from choosing a presentation that is technically organized but unsuitable for the user's workflow. If more than one format could work, ask for the format that best supports a particular use, such as comparison, implementation, or decision-making.

Study instruction:
- "Turn a vague request to organize information into an explicit format instruction with named sections or columns."

Key point:
- Format labels should be supplemented with structure when needed.
- Choose a presentation that supports the user's next action.
23. Q: How can a user specify a useful time period or geographic boundary?
A: Show answer
When time or location affects the answer, name the relevant period or place rather than assuming it is obvious. A question about current market conditions, applicable regulations, or recent events may produce different answers depending on the date. A question about taxes, public services, or local procedures may depend on jurisdiction. Specify whether the request concerns a calendar year, financial year, a particular date range, a country, a state, or a municipality. If the user wants a general explanation rather than a location-specific answer, say so. These details are examples of context that can make a broad request precise.

Study instruction:
- "Review prompts involving current information or rules and add the exact time period and jurisdiction that determine the answer."

Key point:
- Time and place can materially change an answer.
- State the relevant period and jurisdiction instead of leaving them implicit.
24. Q: Why should pronouns and references such as this, that, and it be used carefully?
A: Show answer
Pronouns are convenient when the referent is obvious, but they can create ambiguity when a prompt mentions several people, documents, options, or processes. A request to compare it with the previous one may be unclear if several items have been discussed. Repeating the exact name can make the instruction longer but prevents the model from selecting the wrong referent. When referring to an earlier item, use a clear label such as Option B, the second report, or the 2025 budget forecast. In longer prompts, consistent labels also help maintain continuity between separate instructions.

Study instruction:
- "Find every pronoun in a multi-part prompt and replace any unclear reference with the exact item name or a consistent label."

Key point:
- Unclear references can redirect the task to the wrong subject.
- Use stable labels when several items are being discussed.
25. Q: How can examples in a prompt help clarify the expected response?
A: Show answer
An example can show the kind of detail, structure, tone, or reasoning the user expects. It is especially useful when the desired result is difficult to describe using general instructions alone. For instance, a user can provide one sample row to demonstrate the structure of a comparison table or one sample paragraph to show the preferred level of explanation. The example should be representative and should not contradict the written instructions. It should also be clear whether the model should imitate the format, the reasoning approach, or the content itself. A well-chosen example reduces uncertainty without replacing the need to state the actual task.

Study instruction:
- "Add one representative example to a prompt and state exactly which features should be followed, such as structure, tone, or level of detail."

Key point:
- Examples make abstract expectations more concrete.
- Explain what the example is intended to demonstrate.
26. Q: Can a prompt become less effective by including too many details?
A: Show answer
Yes. Details that do not affect the task can distract from the main request, create competing priorities, and make it harder to identify the instructions that matter. A long prompt is not automatically precise. The useful test is whether each detail changes the subject, context, scope, conditions, audience, or output. Remove unrelated history and repeated instructions, but retain information needed to understand the task correctly. If substantial background is necessary, organize it into clearly labeled sections so that the main task and constraints remain easy to identify.

Study instruction:
- "Edit a lengthy prompt by removing repeated or irrelevant information while preserving every detail that changes the expected result."

Key point:
- More words do not necessarily produce more precision.
- Keep material context and remove distracting repetition.
27. Q: How should a prompt handle several requested tasks at once?
A: Show answer
List the tasks separately and indicate their order or relationship. A request may ask the model to summarize a document, identify risks, and recommend actions; these are distinct operations and should not be left as an undifferentiated instruction. State whether each task is required, whether later tasks depend on earlier findings, and whether the outputs should be presented separately. If the tasks are large or unrelated, dividing them into separate prompts may improve focus. When they belong together, a numbered sequence or clear headings can preserve the overall objective while making each deliverable identifiable.

Study instruction:
- "Convert a multi-task prompt into an ordered list and mark which tasks depend on the results of previous tasks."

Key point:
- Separate distinct actions so none is overlooked.
- State order and dependencies when they affect the work.
28. Q: What should a user do when two prompt instructions appear to contradict each other?
A: Show answer
Review the instructions and identify the exact conflict before expecting the model to resolve it. For example, asking for a one-paragraph response and a detailed explanation of ten separate issues may create incompatible expectations. The user should decide which requirement has priority or revise the instructions so that both can be satisfied, such as requesting a short overview followed by an appendix. Constraints should be internally consistent and should not demand mutually exclusive formats, lengths, or levels of detail. If the priority cannot be determined, the prompt can explicitly ask the model to identify the conflict rather than silently choosing one instruction.

Study instruction:
- "Inspect a prompt for incompatible length, format, source, or scope requirements and rewrite them into a consistent set."

Key point:
- Conflicting instructions make the intended result uncertain.
- Resolve priority explicitly or redesign the requirements so they can coexist.
29. Q: How can a prompt make clear what information should be excluded?
A: Show answer
State exclusions when they define the intended boundary or prevent a common misunderstanding. A user might ask for a summary of operational risks while excluding personal opinions, or request a comparison limited to the supplied options. Exclusions should be specific and relevant; a long list of unrelated prohibitions can make the prompt harder to follow. It is also important not to exclude information necessary for an accurate answer. If a topic has important exceptions or limitations, the user should not prohibit their mention merely to obtain a simpler response. A good exclusion narrows the task without undermining completeness.

Study instruction:
- "Add only those exclusions that protect the intended scope, and check that none would prevent a necessary qualification."

Key point:
- Exclusions can clarify boundaries and prevent unwanted coverage.
- Do not exclude information essential to an accurate result.
30. Q: When should a user ask ChatGPT to state its assumptions?
A: Show answer
Ask for assumptions when the prompt lacks information that may affect the result but the task can still proceed provisionally. The model can then distinguish what was supplied from what it inferred. For example, a planning request may omit the project start date, so an explicitly stated planning assumption can make the draft usable while signaling what must be confirmed. Assumptions should not be treated as verified facts. If an omitted detail would fundamentally change the answer, asking a clarifying question may be safer than proceeding. The prompt should identify which assumptions need to be surfaced and whether provisional alternatives should be shown.

Study instruction:
- "Choose a task with one missing input and ask for any assumptions to be listed separately from supplied facts."

Key point:
- Visible assumptions are easier to review than hidden assumptions.
- Do not use assumptions to replace essential missing information.
31. Q: When is a clarifying question preferable to making a more specific prompt immediately?
A: Show answer
A clarifying question is preferable when an essential choice cannot be inferred safely or when different interpretations would lead to materially different results. For example, a request to prepare a tax explanation without identifying the jurisdiction may require clarification before a reliable answer can be given. If the missing detail is minor, the model may state a reasonable assumption and proceed. The user can make this distinction explicit by instructing the model to ask before proceeding when a required input is absent. Clarification is not a failure of prompting; it is a way to avoid building a detailed answer on an incorrect premise.

Study instruction:
- "Identify which missing details in a sample task are essential and which can be handled through a clearly labeled assumption."

Key point:
- Clarify information that materially changes the answer.
- Proceed with stated assumptions only when that is appropriate for the task.
32. Q: How can a user refine a response that is still too broad?
A: Show answer
Compare the response with the original objective and identify the dimension that remains too broad. The missing direction may concern the subject, audience, time period, scope, level of detail, or output format. A useful follow-up names the part to retain and the part to narrow. For example, ask for the discussion to focus only on implementation risks for a small team rather than requesting a completely new answer without explaining what was wrong. Refinement should add the missing direction, not merely repeat the original prompt. This makes the next response more likely to address the actual gap.

Study instruction:
- "Take a broad answer and write a follow-up that narrows one specific dimension without changing the original objective."

Key point:
- Refinement works best when it identifies the precise gap.
- Preserve useful context while adding the missing direction.
33. Q: What should a user do when a response answers the topic but not the requested question?
A: Show answer
Check whether the prompt clearly identified the action and the exact issue to be answered. A model may discuss a related topic if the request contains a broad subject but does not state the question's focus. The user should restate the required question directly, identify any relevant context, and explain what would count as a complete answer. For example, if the request asks why a process fails but the response only describes the process, the follow-up should ask for causes and evidence rather than another general description. A precise correction is more effective than saying only that the answer is wrong or unhelpful.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a response with its prompt and write a correction that names the unanswered question and the evidence or explanation required."

Key point:
- Topic relevance is not the same as answering the exact question.
- State the missing analytical task directly.
34. Q: How can the quality of a prompt be evaluated before it is submitted?
A: Show answer
A prompt can be reviewed against the intended result. Ask whether the task is explicit, the subject is identifiable, the context is relevant, the scope is bounded, important conditions are stated, and the output format is suitable. Check for ambiguous words, unclear pronouns, repeated instructions, and contradictory constraints. Then imagine two reasonable interpretations of the prompt; if they would lead to substantially different answers, add the detail that distinguishes the intended interpretation. The review should be proportional to the task. A simple request may need only a short check, while a high-impact analytical task deserves a more careful review.

Study instruction:
- "Use the task, subject, context, scope, constraints, format, and ambiguity checks to review a prompt before sending it."

Key point:
- Prompt quality can be tested against the result the user needs.
- Review more carefully when ambiguity could materially affect the outcome.
35. Q: What is a practical checklist for writing a clear and precise prompt?
A: Show answer
A practical checklist asks: What action should be performed? What exact subject is involved? Why is the result needed? Who will use it? Which aspects are in scope? Which conditions or limitations must be respected? What format and level of detail are useful? Is the relevant time period or location specified? Are key terms and references unambiguous? Are the instructions consistent? Not every prompt needs a lengthy answer to every question, but the checklist helps identify omissions that matter. It turns prompt writing into a deliberate communication process rather than a guess about what information the model might need.

Study instruction:
- "Apply the checklist to a real task and remove any question that does not materially affect the expected result."

Key point:
- A short checklist catches common sources of ambiguity.
- Use judgment rather than adding unnecessary detail to every prompt.
36. Q: How can a complex request be divided into manageable prompt stages?
A: Show answer
A complex request can be divided according to its natural workflow: understand the input, analyze it, develop options, evaluate the options, and present a result. Each stage should have a clear output that supports the next stage. For example, a user might first request an inventory of issues in a document, then ask for prioritization, and finally request an action plan. Staging can make the work easier to review and can expose misunderstandings before they spread into later steps. However, the user should preserve the overall objective and important constraints across stages so that the separate outputs remain connected.

Study instruction:
- "Break one complex task into three or four stages and specify the output expected from each stage."

Key point:
- Staging makes complex work easier to inspect and correct.
- Keep the overall objective and constraints consistent across stages.
37. Q: How can a prompt request a comparison that is focused rather than superficial?
A: Show answer
A useful comparison identifies the items being compared and the criteria that matter. Asking which option is better leaves the model to choose its own definition of better. A more precise prompt might request a comparison of cost, implementation time, reliability, and operational risk for a stated purpose. It should also specify whether the answer should identify a preferred option, present trade-offs, or simply describe differences. If evidence is limited, the prompt can ask the model to distinguish supported conclusions from uncertain judgments. Defined criteria make the comparison easier to evaluate and more relevant to the decision.

Study instruction:
- "Rewrite a which is better question by naming the options, decision purpose, evaluation criteria, and required conclusion format."

Key point:
- Comparison quality depends on clear criteria.
- State whether the task is to describe differences or recommend an option.
38. Q: How can a prompt request practical steps instead of a general explanation?
A: Show answer
Use an action-oriented instruction and specify the starting point and desired outcome. A request for an explanation may produce concepts without telling the user what to do. If practical guidance is required, ask for ordered steps, required inputs, decision points, checks, and expected results. Add any relevant constraints such as available resources or the user's level of experience. For a process that includes risk, ask what to do when a step fails or information is missing. This helps connect the explanation to actual execution rather than leaving the user to translate general principles into a procedure.

Study instruction:
- "Turn a general how does this work question into a request for ordered actions, inputs, checks, and exception handling."

Key point:
- State when the desired output is an actionable procedure.
- Include decision points and relevant exceptions when they affect execution.
39. Q: How can a prompt make a request for an overview more useful?
A: Show answer
An overview becomes more useful when the prompt identifies the dimensions that should be covered. A user might request a high-level explanation of a subject that includes its purpose, main components, practical uses, and limitations. This is more directed than asking for everything about the subject, which may encourage excessive breadth. The prompt can also specify the reader's background and the desired length. If the overview is intended to prepare for a decision or later study, state that purpose so the model can emphasize the most relevant concepts. A good overview is selective, not merely long.

Study instruction:
- "Write an overview prompt that names four required dimensions and the audience who will use the result."

Key point:
- An overview needs a defined coverage plan.
- Purpose and audience help determine what deserves emphasis.
40. Q: How should a user specify a request for a summary?
A: Show answer
A summary prompt should identify the material to summarize, the intended reader, the desired length or level of compression, and the information that must be retained. Some summaries should preserve conclusions and recommendations; others should emphasize events, arguments, evidence, or action items. If the user needs a summary for a particular purpose, such as a management briefing or revision notes, that purpose should be stated. It can also be useful to ask for uncertainty or missing information to be identified rather than silently omitted. These instructions make the summary more suitable for its intended use.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare two summary prompts for the same source: one for a senior decision-maker and one for a learner preparing for an examination."

Key point:
- A summary should be designed for its audience and purpose.
- Specify which information must be preserved.
41. Q: How can a prompt avoid asking for an answer that is broader than the available information supports?
A: Show answer
The prompt should identify the information that may be used and ask the model to distinguish supported conclusions from assumptions or unknowns. If the task is based on a supplied document or dataset, say whether outside information is permitted. Ask the model to identify gaps rather than fill them with invented details. The user should also specify whether estimates, illustrative examples, or provisional assumptions are acceptable. This does not guarantee correctness, so important outputs still require review, but it makes the evidence boundary clearer. A precise prompt describes not only the desired result but also the basis on which that result should be produced.

Study instruction:
- "Draft a source-bounded prompt that states which materials may be used and how missing evidence should be reported."

Key point:
- Define the evidence base when unsupported additions would be problematic.
- Ask for gaps and assumptions to be made visible.
42. Q: How can a prompt help prevent the model from confusing an example with a required fact?
A: Show answer
Label examples as illustrative and distinguish them from facts or conditions supplied by the user. If a prompt includes a sample scenario, state whether the model should reuse its structure, its figures, or its underlying facts. This is important because examples are often included to demonstrate format rather than to establish what happened in the actual case. A prompt can also instruct the model not to treat hypothetical numbers or names as real evidence. Clear labeling helps preserve the difference between an illustration and the user's actual situation.

Study instruction:
- "Add labels such as illustrative example, supplied fact, and assumption to a prompt that contains mixed information."

Key point:
- Examples demonstrate an approach but do not automatically establish facts.
- Label hypothetical and verified information distinctly.
43. Q: What should be done when a prompt uses a word such as best, simple, or comprehensive?
A: Show answer
These words should be translated into criteria when their meaning affects the result. Best may mean lowest cost, highest reliability, easiest implementation, or strongest overall fit. Simple may mean few steps, minimal technical language, or low resource requirements. Comprehensive may mean covering specified dimensions rather than discussing every conceivable detail. The prompt should explain the intended meaning or name the criteria that should guide the response. If several interpretations are acceptable, the user can ask for alternatives and explain the trade-offs. Defining evaluative words prevents hidden assumptions from determining the answer.

Study instruction:
- "Select three subjective words in a prompt and replace each with observable criteria or a clear description of the intended meaning."

Key point:
- Subjective terms can conceal different evaluation standards.
- Define the criteria behind words such as best or simple.
44. Q: How can a user make a prompt more actionable by stating the desired outcome?
A: Show answer
The desired outcome explains what the user intends to do with the response. A prompt may request information for learning, planning, decision-making, troubleshooting, communication, or implementation. These purposes affect which details should be emphasized. For example, a request to understand a process may need definitions and a conceptual explanation, while a request to implement it may need ordered steps, responsibilities, checks, and exception handling. Stating the outcome helps the model prioritize information that supports the user's next action. It also provides a basis for judging whether the answer is useful.

Study instruction:
- "Add a sentence beginning with I will use this result to to clarify the purpose of a real prompt."

Key point:
- The intended use helps determine what information is relevant.
- A clear outcome makes the response easier to evaluate.
45. Q: How should a user handle a prompt that needs both a broad explanation and a specific answer?
A: Show answer
Separate the two requirements and state their order. The user can ask for a brief overview to establish context, followed by a direct answer to the specific question. Alternatively, the user can ask for the direct answer first and supporting explanation afterward. This prevents the broad discussion from obscuring the main point. The prompt should specify how much space each part should receive if that balance matters. When the specific answer depends on assumptions or exceptions, those should be explained without allowing the overview to become unrelated background.

Study instruction:
- "Write a prompt that requests a short overview followed by a direct answer, supporting reasoning, and relevant limitations."

Key point:
- Separate context-setting from the specific question.
- State the order when the direct answer must remain prominent.
46. Q: What is a common mistake when trying to make a prompt more precise?
A: Show answer
A common mistake is adding many details without deciding which ones actually guide the answer. This can produce a long prompt that still leaves the main task unclear. Another mistake is narrowing the topic so aggressively that essential context or relevant exceptions are excluded. Users may also add subjective instructions such as make it perfect without explaining the criteria. Precision requires deliberate selection: identify the task, include the information that changes the result, set useful boundaries, and remove repetition. The final prompt should be easier to interpret than the original, not merely longer.

Study instruction:
- "Compare an original prompt with an expanded version and remove any added words that do not clarify task, context, scope, constraints, or format."

Key point:
- Precision is not measured by word count.
- Keep details that guide the result and remove those that do not.
47. Q: How can the dog, cake, and World War II examples be used as a general prompt-writing exercise?
A: Show answer
Each example begins with a broad topic and then adds details that define the requested answer. The dog example narrows the subject to Labrador Retrievers and focuses on diet and common health issues. The cake example specifies a gluten-free chocolate cake, eight servings, and a request for steps and ingredients. The World War II example identifies major causes and asks how they contributed to the outbreak in 1939. Together, the examples demonstrate three useful moves: narrow the subject, add conditions that affect the answer, and name the analytical task. Users can apply the same moves to business, academic, technical, and personal requests.

Study instruction:
- "Create one vague prompt in each of three domains and improve it by narrowing the subject, adding relevant conditions, and specifying the requested analysis."

Key point:
- Specificity can come from the subject, conditions, and task.
- The same improvement method applies across different domains.
48. Q: How can a user decide whether to provide a format example or simply name the format?
A: Show answer
Naming the format is usually sufficient when the format is familiar and its structure is straightforward. An example is more useful when the expected layout, tone, level of detail, or relationship among sections is difficult to describe. For instance, asking for a table may be enough for a simple comparison, but a sample row may be needed when every row must contain several specific fields. The user should avoid providing an example that contains irrelevant facts or conflicts with the requested output. If an example is supplied, identify which features are mandatory and which are only illustrative.

Study instruction:
- "Choose a task with a standard format and one with a specialized format, then decide where a sample output would add useful clarity."

Key point:
- Use a format label for familiar structures.
- Use a representative example when the required structure is specialized.
49. Q: How can prompt clarity be maintained when the request is revised several times?
A: Show answer
Keep the core objective, subject, and non-negotiable constraints visible while changing only the part that needs revision. If the user changes the audience or output format, the prompt should still preserve the original task unless the task itself has changed. Repeating all previous instructions can create clutter, but removing a constraint that remains important can produce an inconsistent result. A concise revised prompt can state what remains unchanged and what is different. For a long task, a short list of confirmed requirements can help prevent accidental drift between versions.

Study instruction:
- "Revise a multi-part prompt by marking the unchanged requirements and the single dimension being updated."

Key point:
- A revision should not silently discard important requirements.
- State what changes and what remains constant.
50. Q: What should a user do if a prompt produces a response in the wrong format?
A: Show answer
First determine whether the original prompt named the required format and whether it described the necessary structure. If it did not, add a direct format instruction and identify the parts the output must contain. If it did, restate the requirement in a concise correction and point out the specific mismatch. For example, ask for the same analysis to be reorganized into a table with named columns rather than asking for a completely different analysis. The user should also check whether other instructions conflict with the requested format. A format correction should preserve the substantive task unless the user intends to change it.

Study instruction:
- "Write a follow-up prompt that corrects a format mismatch while preserving the original subject and analytical requirements."

Key point:
- Correct the presentation explicitly and specifically.
- Do not change the task unintentionally when correcting its format.
51. Q: How can a user apply clarity and precision when asking for a recommendation?
A: Show answer
A recommendation prompt should identify the decision, the available options, the objective, and the criteria that matter. It should also provide relevant constraints such as budget, time, resources, or risk tolerance when those factors affect the choice. The user can ask for a recommended option, the reasons supporting it, the main trade-offs, and the circumstances that could change the recommendation. Without criteria, the model may choose an option based on assumptions that do not match the user's priorities. A precise recommendation prompt makes the basis for the advice visible and easier to challenge.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a recommendation prompt naming the decision, options, evaluation criteria, constraints, and required explanation of trade-offs."

Key point:
- Recommendations depend on the user's objective and decision criteria.
- Ask for trade-offs and conditions that could change the preferred option.
52. Q: How can clarity and precision improve a prompt for troubleshooting help?
A: Show answer
A troubleshooting prompt should describe the observed problem, the expected behavior, the circumstances in which the problem occurs, and any steps already attempted. Include relevant error messages or examples when available, while avoiding unrelated background. State the environment or version if it could change the solution. Ask for a diagnostic sequence that begins with the safest or most informative checks and explains what each result would indicate. This gives the model a defined problem to investigate instead of asking it to guess what is wrong. If key information is missing, the prompt can invite targeted clarifying questions.

Study instruction:
- "Rewrite a vague help request by adding the observed symptom, expected result, context, prior attempts, and desired diagnostic format."

Key point:
- Troubleshooting requires a clear description of the actual problem.
- Relevant evidence and context reduce guesswork.
53. Q: How can clarity and precision improve a prompt for learning a new subject?
A: Show answer
A learning prompt should identify the subject, the learner's current level, the learning objective, and the preferred teaching approach. A beginner may need definitions and simple examples, while an experienced learner may need comparisons, applications, and limitations. The user can ask for a sequence that moves from foundational concepts to practical exercises, or request a short explanation followed by questions to test understanding. Naming the intended outcome, such as preparing for an examination or applying a concept at work, helps prioritize the material. A focused learning prompt is more useful than a broad request to teach everything about a subject.

Study instruction:
- "Create a learning prompt for a named audience and objective, specifying the sequence and practice activities required."

Key point:
- Learning prompts should reflect the learner's level and goal.
- A defined progression makes study material more purposeful.
54. Q: How can a prompt request a useful explanation without making the answer unnecessarily long?
A: Show answer
State the essential aspects to cover and the intended level of detail. A user can request a concise explanation that includes a definition, the main principle, one practical example, and a key limitation. This gives the model a coverage target while discouraging unrelated expansion. If a length limit is important, specify it as a practical target and identify which information must not be omitted. A short answer can still be precise when its boundaries are clear. Conversely, a strict word limit may be unsuitable when the subject requires qualifications or safety-critical detail.

Study instruction:
- "Write a concise explanation prompt with a defined audience, four required elements, and a realistic length target."

Key point:
- Conciseness works best when essential coverage is specified.
- Do not impose a limit that would remove necessary qualifications.
55. Q: How can a prompt make the relationship between facts, analysis, and recommendations clear?
A: Show answer
Ask for the response to distinguish the information supplied, the analysis based on that information, and any recommendations that follow. This is useful when the user needs to review how a conclusion was reached. The prompt can request that facts be summarized first, assumptions or uncertainties identified next, and recommendations presented with their supporting reasons. If the task is based on a source document, the user can ask for claims to be tied to the relevant source material. This structure makes it easier to detect when a recommendation goes beyond the evidence or when a missing fact affects the conclusion.

Study instruction:
- "Draft a prompt requiring separate treatment of supplied facts, analysis, assumptions, and recommendations."

Key point:
- Separate evidence from interpretation and advice.
- A clear structure makes reasoning easier to review.
56. Q: What final test can show whether a prompt is ready to submit?
A: Show answer
Imagine that a capable person who has not heard the surrounding conversation receives only the prompt. Could that person identify the task, subject, purpose, relevant context, scope, constraints, and expected output? Would two reasonable readers understand the key terms and references in substantially the same way? Are any requirements contradictory, and are important conditions missing? If the answer is clear enough for the task, the prompt is ready. If not, add the missing direction or ask a clarifying question. This final test reflects the chapter's central lesson: effective prompting is a form of precise communication.

Study instruction:
- "Give the prompt to a colleague without explaining it verbally and ask them to describe the task and expected output; revise any point they interpret differently."

Key point:
- A prompt is ready when its intended task and result are clear without hidden explanation.
- Clarity and precision are practical communication skills that improve through review and revision.
Rows: 56
Table 3
Prompting for Detailed and Comprehensive Responses in ChatGPT
1. Q: How can a user prompt ChatGPT to produce a detailed and comprehensive response?
A: Show answer
A user should state explicitly that depth is required and describe what a complete response should cover. A request such as “explain this in detail” signals a preference, but a stronger prompt identifies the subject, purpose, audience, scope, relevant dimensions, and expected structure. For example, a request for a comprehensive explanation of electric vehicles can specify environmental benefits and costs, lifecycle emissions, battery materials, electricity sources, manufacturing, end-of-life management, and practical examples. The prompt should also indicate whether the response should compare perspectives, explain mechanisms, identify limitations, or provide recommendations. Explicit instructions reduce the chance that the model will interpret a broad question as a request for a short overview. They do not guarantee completeness or factual accuracy, so important claims still require review and verification.

Study instruction:
- "Rewrite a broad question as a detailed task specification that identifies scope, depth, audience, structure, and the dimensions that must be addressed."

Key point:
- State the required depth and define what comprehensive coverage means for the particular task.
- A detailed instruction improves direction but does not replace checking the answer.
2. Q: What is the difference between asking for a detailed answer and asking for a comprehensive answer?
A: Show answer
A detailed answer explains selected points with sufficient depth, including relevant mechanisms, reasons, examples, and qualifications. A comprehensive answer aims for appropriate breadth as well as depth: it covers the major dimensions of the subject rather than examining only one or two aspects. The terms overlap, but they are not identical. A detailed explanation of battery chemistry may be technically deep while omitting manufacturing impacts, electricity generation, recycling, and policy considerations. A comprehensive overview should identify those broader dimensions and explain the most important ones at a suitable level. A prompt should therefore specify whether the user wants deep analysis of a narrow issue, broad coverage of a whole subject, or both. The appropriate balance depends on the task, available evidence, audience, and practical limits on response length.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a narrow technical explanation with a broad subject overview and identify which dimensions each one includes or excludes."

Key point:
- Detail concerns depth within a topic; comprehensiveness concerns adequate coverage of its relevant dimensions.
- Specify both when the task requires breadth and depth.
3. Q: Why should a prompt explicitly identify the intended audience when requesting a detailed response?
A: Show answer
Audience information helps determine the level of technical language, assumed background knowledge, examples, and explanatory detail. A response for a beginner may need definitions, analogies, and a gradual explanation of foundational concepts. A response for a specialist may be more useful when it emphasizes assumptions, technical distinctions, evidence, limitations, and advanced implications. A manager may need decision criteria and operational consequences, while a student may need conceptual explanations and practice questions. Without audience information, the model must infer the reader's knowledge and may produce an answer that is either too elementary or too technical. The prompt should describe the reader's role or knowledge level without assuming that an audience label alone is sufficient; the user should also identify the purpose for which the information will be used.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare three versions of the same question for a beginner, a technical specialist, and a decision-maker, then compare their required explanations."

Key point:
- Audience affects vocabulary, depth, examples, and emphasis.
- State both who will use the answer and what they need to accomplish.
4. Q: How does providing context improve the specificity and usefulness of a ChatGPT response?
A: Show answer
Context supplies information needed to interpret the request correctly. It may include the background, objective, relevant facts, constraints, intended audience, prior decisions, terminology, or circumstances surrounding a problem. Without context, a question may have several plausible interpretations and the answer may rely on assumptions that do not fit the user's situation. For example, asking how to improve a reporting process is broad; explaining the reporting cycle, users, source systems, recurring errors, deadlines, and approval responsibilities makes the task more concrete. Context should be relevant and organized rather than an indiscriminate collection of details. Users should distinguish confirmed facts from assumptions and identify information that must not be inferred. Sensitive or confidential information should be minimized or appropriately anonymized before it is included in a prompt.

Study instruction:
- "Take a generic request and add only the contextual facts that materially change the analysis, recommendation, or output."

Key point:
- Relevant context narrows interpretation and supports more tailored reasoning.
- Unnecessary or inaccurate context can distract or misdirect the response.
5. Q: What types of context should be supplied when asking ChatGPT to analyze a complex problem?
A: Show answer
Useful context commonly includes the problem statement, desired outcome, background events, affected stakeholders, available evidence, constraints, relevant definitions, time horizon, and decisions already made. Depending on the task, the user may also provide data, examples, policies, technical specifications, or a description of the current process. It is important to identify what is known, what remains uncertain, and which assumptions are provisional. The prompt should explain why the issue matters and what form of assistance is expected: diagnosis, explanation, comparison, risk assessment, planning, or drafting. Context should be structured so that the model can distinguish the task from supporting information. Where the source material is incomplete, the user should ask the model to identify gaps rather than silently fill them with invented details.

Study instruction:
- "Build a context checklist for one complex task and label each item as a fact, assumption, constraint, or open question."

Key point:
- Context should explain the situation, evidence, constraints, and intended outcome.
- Explicitly separate facts from assumptions and missing information.
6. Q: How can follow-up questions be used to obtain deeper information from ChatGPT?
A: Show answer
Follow-up questions develop an initial response through successive clarification and analysis. A user can ask the model to explain a term, expand a mechanism, provide a worked example, examine an exception, compare alternatives, test an assumption, or identify practical implications. This iterative approach is useful because a broad first response can reveal which areas deserve further investigation. Follow-ups should refer clearly to the relevant part of the previous answer and state what additional understanding is required. If the discussion changes direction, the user should restate the new objective and any assumptions that should no longer apply. Follow-up questions can improve depth, but they can also compound an earlier error if the initial answer is accepted uncritically. Important claims should therefore be checked against reliable evidence before the conversation builds further conclusions on them.

Study instruction:
- "Start with a broad question and prepare a sequence of follow-ups covering definition, mechanism, example, limitation, and application."

Key point:
- Follow-ups turn an initial overview into a progressively deeper inquiry.
- Verify foundational claims before relying on them in later analysis.
7. Q: What makes a follow-up question effective rather than repetitive?
A: Show answer
An effective follow-up adds a new analytical requirement or resolves a specific uncertainty. It may ask for a causal explanation, a different perspective, a practical example, a comparison, a boundary condition, or evidence supporting a claim. A repetitive follow-up merely requests the same explanation in slightly different words without identifying what was missing. Before asking, the user should determine whether the previous response failed to define a concept, explain a relationship, address a constraint, or demonstrate an application. A precise follow-up names that gap. For example, after receiving an overview of electric-vehicle impacts, the user might ask how the electricity mix changes lifecycle emissions, rather than simply requesting more detail about environmental impacts. This makes the next response more focused and helps prevent unnecessary expansion.

Study instruction:
- "Review an initial answer and write three follow-ups, each targeting a different unresolved issue rather than repeating the original request."

Key point:
- A strong follow-up identifies a meaningful gap in the existing explanation.
- Ask for a new dimension, not merely a longer repetition.
8. Q: When should a user use an open-ended question to obtain a detailed response?
A: Show answer
Open-ended questions are appropriate when the user needs explanation, analysis, exploration, or multiple relevant perspectives. They invite the model to develop an answer rather than select from a narrow set of responses. A question such as “What are the implications of blockchain technology for data security?” allows discussion of architecture, access control, integrity, privacy, governance, threats, and limitations. Open-ended questions are particularly useful at the exploratory stage when the user does not yet know all the dimensions that should be considered. However, openness should not become vagueness. The prompt should still define the subject, purpose, scope, and any important boundaries. Otherwise, the response may be broad but shallow, or may spend space on aspects that are not relevant to the user's objective.

Study instruction:
- "Convert a yes-or-no question into an open-ended inquiry that asks for mechanisms, implications, examples, and limitations."

Key point:
- Open-ended questions encourage explanation and exploration.
- Add scope and purpose so that breadth remains relevant.
9. Q: What are the advantages and limitations of closed questions when interacting with ChatGPT?
A: Show answer
Closed questions usually seek a bounded answer, such as yes or no, a date, a category, or confirmation of a specific fact. They are useful when the user needs a quick check, a precise data point, or a decision within clearly defined alternatives. Their limitation is that they may not reveal the reasoning, qualifications, or context behind the answer. Asking “Is blockchain secure?” encourages an oversimplified conclusion because security depends on the design, implementation, threat model, governance, and use case. A more useful closed question might ask whether a specified system uses a particular consensus mechanism, followed by an open-ended request to explain its security implications. Closed questions should therefore be used for focused information gathering, not as a substitute for analysis when the issue is complex or conditional.

Study instruction:
- "Identify when a closed question is suitable for fact confirmation and when it needs an explanatory follow-up."

Key point:
- Closed questions are efficient for bounded facts and confirmations.
- Complex issues usually require qualifications and explanatory follow-ups.
10. Q: How can open-ended and closed questions be combined within one ChatGPT conversation?
A: Show answer
A useful sequence often begins with an open-ended question to map the subject, uses closed questions to confirm specific details, and returns to open-ended questions to examine implications. For example, a user exploring electric vehicles might first ask for a balanced overview of environmental impacts. The user can then confirm a specific technical point, such as whether battery production contributes to manufacturing emissions, and follow with an open-ended question about how battery chemistry, electricity generation, and recycling affect lifecycle outcomes. The sequence moves from exploration to verification and then to synthesis. The user should make each transition explicit so that the model understands whether it is being asked to explain, confirm, compare, or evaluate. This combination supports both conversational flow and analytical depth.

Study instruction:
- "Design a five-question sequence that alternates broad exploration, focused confirmation, and deeper evaluation."

Key point:
- Open-ended questions explore; closed questions confirm bounded details.
- Combining them creates a structured path from overview to analysis.
11. Q: How does specificity in question wording reduce generic ChatGPT answers?
A: Show answer
Specificity narrows the subject and identifies the exact issue the response should address. A broad request such as “Explain technology” leaves the model to choose the domain, level, and angle. A more specific question identifies the technology, use case, affected stakeholders, and desired analysis. Specific wording helps distinguish closely related concepts and reduces the likelihood that the answer will consist mainly of general statements. It can also improve relevance by identifying the context in which a principle should be applied. Specificity should not be confused with excessive detail: a prompt can become difficult to follow if it includes too many unrelated requirements. The objective is to include the details that materially affect the answer while maintaining a clear central question and a manageable scope.

Study instruction:
- "Rewrite three generic questions by specifying the subject, setting, intended analysis, and expected level of detail."

Key point:
- Specific wording directs attention toward the actual problem.
- Include material details without turning the prompt into an unstructured list of demands.
12. Q: How can a prompt request examples that make a detailed explanation more concrete?
A: Show answer
The prompt should state what kind of example is useful and what the example should demonstrate. A request may ask for a realistic workplace scenario, a numerical illustration, a simplified analogy, a step-by-step demonstration, or a comparison of contrasting cases. It should also identify any constraints, such as the industry, audience, scale, or assumptions to use. For example, asking for an illustration of a data-security principle becomes more useful when the user requests a scenario involving a small organization, identifies the data at risk, and asks for the control failure and corrective action. Examples should clarify the concept rather than introduce unrelated details. If numerical values or factual events are illustrative, the response should label them as such so they are not mistaken for verified real-world data.

Study instruction:
- "For one abstract concept, request a definition, a realistic scenario, a contrasting example, and an explanation of what each example demonstrates."

Key point:
- Specify the type and purpose of the example needed.
- Distinguish illustrative assumptions from verified facts.
13. Q: What is the value of requesting both positive and negative aspects in a prompt?
A: Show answer
Asking for both benefits and drawbacks encourages a balanced examination instead of a one-sided description. It prompts consideration of trade-offs, unintended consequences, implementation costs, risks, and conditions under which a claimed benefit may not materialize. In the chapter's electric-vehicle example, reduced tailpipe pollution is considered alongside battery-material extraction, water impacts, and manufacturing emissions. A balanced prompt should not imply that every positive and negative factor has equal importance; the answer should assess the evidence, scale, uncertainty, and context of each factor. Users can improve the request further by asking which conclusions are well established, which depend on assumptions, and which require additional data. This helps distinguish a balanced analysis from a superficial list of pros and cons.

Study instruction:
- "Choose a technology or policy and prepare a prompt that requires benefits, costs, distributional effects, uncertainties, and conditions."

Key point:
- Explicitly request countervailing effects to reduce one-sided analysis.
- Balance requires evaluating significance and evidence, not simply listing opposing points.
14. Q: How should a user ask ChatGPT to explain the causes and consequences of a complex issue?
A: Show answer
The prompt should distinguish causes, contributing factors, immediate triggers, mechanisms, and consequences. These are not interchangeable. An immediate trigger may precede an event without fully explaining why the event occurred, while a contributing factor may increase the likelihood or severity of an outcome without being sufficient by itself. The user can request a causal chain, identify alternative explanations, and ask which links are supported by evidence. Consequences should be examined across relevant time horizons and stakeholder groups. For example, a prompt about a historical event can ask for the political, economic, social, and institutional conditions that shaped it, followed by its short- and long-term effects. The answer should avoid presenting correlation as causation or treating a single factor as the complete explanation when multiple mechanisms are plausible.

Study instruction:
- "Map a complex issue into background conditions, immediate triggers, causal mechanisms, alternative explanations, and consequences."

Key point:
- Separate triggers from deeper causes and contributing conditions.
- Ask for evidence and alternative explanations before accepting a causal conclusion.
15. Q: How can a prompt elicit a step-by-step explanation of a process?
A: Show answer
A step-by-step prompt should identify the process to be explained, its starting point, expected end state, relevant actors, and the level of operational detail required. It can ask for prerequisites, sequence, decision points, inputs, outputs, checks, and exceptions at each stage. For a technical process, the user may also request commands, formulas, or a worked example, but should specify the platform and version when those details matter. A useful process explanation does more than list actions: it explains why each step is performed and what evidence indicates that it was completed correctly. Users should request warnings about common failure points and clarify whether the explanation is conceptual or intended for direct implementation. Instructions for high-risk activities should be independently checked against authoritative procedures before use.

Study instruction:
- "Select a process and request its prerequisites, ordered steps, decision points, outputs, verification checks, and exception handling."

Key point:
- A process prompt should define the start, end, actors, sequence, and controls.
- Ask what confirms that each important step was completed correctly.
16. Q: How can ChatGPT be prompted to compare two or more alternatives meaningfully?
A: Show answer
The prompt should identify the alternatives and the criteria that matter to the decision. Relevant criteria may include cost, performance, risk, reliability, complexity, implementation time, scalability, legal constraints, or suitability for a particular user. Without common criteria, a comparison may describe each option separately without helping the reader judge the differences. The user should state whether criteria have equal importance or whether some are decisive. It is also useful to request assumptions, trade-offs, uncertainties, and circumstances in which each alternative is preferable. If information is unavailable for one option, the answer should identify the gap rather than invent a comparable value. A comparison should conclude with conditional findings tied to the stated criteria, not an unsupported universal ranking.

Study instruction:
- "Create a comparison prompt that defines alternatives, common criteria, priority weights, missing information, and conditions for choosing each option."

Key point:
- Meaningful comparisons use consistent criteria and explicit priorities.
- A conditional conclusion is more reliable than an unsupported claim that one option is always best.
17. Q: How should a user request a comprehensive overview of a historical event and its modern influence?
A: Show answer
The prompt should define the event or period, the geographical and chronological scope, and the aspects of modern influence to be examined. It can request the historical background, major actors, sequence of events, competing interpretations, institutional changes, and examples of present-day effects. The chapter's French Revolution example illustrates how a historical event can be connected to ideas such as liberty, equality, and fraternity and to later political systems. A careful prompt should ask the model to distinguish direct historical influence from later reinterpretation or indirect influence. It should also request credible sources and identify areas where historians disagree. This prevents a simplified narrative from implying that a single event created a modern institution or political arrangement by itself.

Study instruction:
- "Write a historical-analysis prompt that separates chronology, causes, contemporary interpretations, long-term influence, and contested claims."

Key point:
- Historical analysis requires chronology and context as well as modern examples.
- Distinguish direct influence from later interpretation and oversimplified causal claims.
18. Q: How can a prompt request a comprehensive overview of advances in medical technology without producing an unstructured list?
A: Show answer
The prompt should define the clinical or operational objective and group technologies by their contribution to care. The chapter's example mentions telemedicine, artificial intelligence in diagnostics, and wearable devices. A more structured request can ask how each technology works, which patient or provider need it addresses, what evidence supports its use, what limitations exist, and what implementation risks must be managed. It should distinguish improved access, diagnostic performance, monitoring, treatment, and administrative efficiency because these outcomes are not the same. The prompt should also request discussion of privacy, safety, clinical oversight, equity, and regulatory requirements where relevant. Recent developments require current evidence, so users should ask for dated authoritative sources and verify that cited technologies and approvals remain current.

Study instruction:
- "Organize a technology overview by intended clinical outcome and require evidence, limitations, safety considerations, and implementation conditions for each category."

Key point:
- Structure technology analysis around the problem each innovation is intended to solve.
- Separate demonstrated clinical benefit from potential or emerging capability.
19. Q: What should a user do when a broad prompt produces a response that is still too generic?
A: Show answer
The user should identify exactly what is missing rather than merely repeating the original request. The gap may concern technical depth, examples, a specific audience, a relevant jurisdiction, a particular time period, practical steps, or evidence. A revised prompt can narrow the scope and ask for concrete mechanisms, assumptions, and implications. The user may also provide a representative example of the level of detail expected. If the response is generic because the model lacks essential facts, the user should supply those facts or ask which information is needed. If the subject is inherently broad, divide it into manageable subtopics and explore them sequentially. This targeted revision is more efficient than repeatedly asking for “more detail” without defining what a useful improvement would contain.

Study instruction:
- "Diagnose a generic answer by identifying its missing scope, evidence, examples, assumptions, or operational detail, then write a targeted revision prompt."

Key point:
- Diagnose the specific deficiency before rewriting the prompt.
- Narrow or subdivide the task when breadth is preventing useful depth.
20. Q: How can a user prevent a detailed prompt from becoming confusing or overloaded?
A: Show answer
A detailed prompt should be organized around one clear objective, with supporting requirements grouped into logical categories. The user can separate background, task, scope, constraints, output format, and quality criteria using labels or short sections. Requirements should be prioritized so that essential instructions are distinguishable from optional preferences. Conflicting directions should be resolved before submission; for example, requesting both a one-paragraph answer and a comprehensive technical report creates an avoidable ambiguity. Long prompts should avoid unrelated background and repeated instructions. If the task contains several independent deliverables, it may be more effective to sequence them into stages. The user should preserve necessary context while making the requested action easy to identify, and can ask the model to state any ambiguity before proceeding when interaction is available.

Study instruction:
- "Restructure a long prompt into objective, context, scope, constraints, deliverables, and quality criteria; remove repeated or conflicting requirements."

Key point:
- Organization makes detailed instructions easier to interpret.
- Prioritize requirements and resolve contradictions before submission.
21. Q: How can a prompt specify the expected structure of a comprehensive answer?
A: Show answer
The prompt should describe the sections or sequence that will make the answer useful. Depending on the task, the user may request an executive overview, definitions, explanation, examples, comparison, risks, limitations, practical steps, and conclusion. For a decision analysis, a structure might include the problem, options, evaluation criteria, evidence, trade-offs, recommendation conditions, and implementation steps. The structure should reflect the logic of the subject rather than impose headings that add no value. If a table, checklist, numbered procedure, or narrative is required, the prompt should state that explicitly and clarify any constraints on length or format. Specifying structure improves consistency and makes omissions easier to detect, but it does not itself ensure that the content is accurate or complete.

Study instruction:
- "Choose an output structure suited to a research, comparison, process, or decision task and explain why each requested section is needed."

Key point:
- Define the output structure according to the intended use of the answer.
- Review the final response for both structural compliance and substantive completeness.
22. Q: How should users request evidence and source attribution when they need a reliable detailed answer?
A: Show answer
The prompt should ask for sources that directly support material factual claims and should identify the preferred source hierarchy.
