Table 5
Technical and Informational Prompting: Accuracy, Context, and Evidence
1. Q: What is technical and informational prompting, and what makes it different from creative prompting?
A: Show answer
Technical and informational prompting is the practice of framing a question so that an AI system returns factual, relevant, and appropriately detailed information. Unlike a creative request, where the user may welcome surprise and interpretation, a technical or factual request usually has a narrower target and a stronger need for accuracy. The prompt should identify the exact subject, the level of explanation, the relevant context, the assumptions that apply, and the evidence or references expected. The chapter emphasizes that the model needs to understand not only what topic is being discussed but also what the user needs to know about it and how the answer will be used. A fluent answer is not sufficient if it is incomplete, imprecise, or unsupported.

Study instruction:
- Compare a creative prompt with a factual prompt on the same topic and identify which instructions are needed to make the factual answer verifiable.

Key point:
- Technical prompts prioritize precision, relevance, and support for factual claims.
- The prompt should make the intended scope and evidence standard explicit.

Source links:
- OpenAI: Prompt engineering
2. Q: Why should a technical prompt state the desired level of detail?
A: Show answer
The same question can require a short overview, a practical explanation, or a detailed technical treatment. If the prompt does not specify the level, the model must guess how much background to include and which concepts to explain. A beginner may need definitions, analogies, and a step-by-step description; an experienced practitioner may need assumptions, mechanisms, edge cases, and implementation trade-offs. Stating the level also helps manage length and terminology. A useful instruction identifies both the depth and the purpose, such as “give a concise executive overview” or “provide a detailed explanation suitable for a second-year engineering student.” The answer should be deep enough for the task without adding irrelevant complexity.

Study instruction:
- Rewrite one question for a beginner, a specialist, and a decision-maker; compare the expected depth and vocabulary.

Key point:
- Detail level controls complexity, background, and answer length.
- Match depth to the reader’s knowledge and intended use.

Source links:
- OpenAI: Prompt engineering
3. Q: How can a prompt distinguish between a high-level overview and a detailed technical explanation?
A: Show answer
Describe the expected components of the answer rather than relying only on labels such as “basic” or “advanced.” A high-level overview might require the main purpose, core components, benefits, limitations, and one simple example. A detailed explanation may require underlying mechanisms, terminology, operating steps, dependencies, assumptions, failure modes, and trade-offs. The prompt can also set boundaries by saying what should be excluded, such as mathematical derivations or implementation code. This makes the requested level easier to interpret and evaluate. If the user is unsure which level is appropriate, the prompt can request a short overview first and then a separate deeper explanation of the most important mechanism.

Study instruction:
- Create a two-column checklist of what belongs in an overview and what belongs in a technical deep dive for one subject.

Key point:
- Define depth through expected content, not vague labels alone.
- Exclusions can be as important as requested details.

Source links:
- OpenAI: Prompt engineering
4. Q: How should a user specify the audience or expertise level for a technical answer?
A: Show answer
Identify who will use the answer and what they are likely to know already. “Explain for a non-specialist manager,” “assume familiarity with basic networking,” and “write for a graduate student in computer science” imply different starting points. If the audience has mixed expertise, ask for a short plain-language explanation followed by a technical section. Avoid relying on job titles alone because people with the same title may have different knowledge. A useful prompt can state prerequisite concepts, preferred terminology, and whether acronyms should be defined. Audience information helps the model choose appropriate examples and avoid either unexplained jargon or excessive simplification.

Study instruction:
- Take a technical question and rewrite it for a general reader and for a specialist, noting which prerequisites each version assumes.

Key point:
- Audience determines the starting level and explanatory language.
- State prerequisites and acronym expectations when they matter.

Source links:
- OpenAI: Prompt engineering
5. Q: Why is precise technical language important when asking for factual information?
A: Show answer
Technical terms narrow the meaning of a question and help distinguish concepts that may sound similar in ordinary language. Asking about “photovoltaic cells” is more focused than asking generally how solar panels work, because it directs attention to the component and mechanism of interest. Precision also reduces the risk that the answer will spend most of its space on a broad topic instead of the requested issue. However, technical vocabulary should be used only when the user understands it or can define it. If a term is ambiguous or used differently across fields, specify the intended meaning. Precision improves focus; it does not replace the need for context or source verification.

Study instruction:
- Replace three broad everyday terms with the precise technical terms relevant to the intended question, and define each term briefly.

Key point:
- Specific terminology narrows the scope of a factual request.
- Define ambiguous terms instead of assuming one interpretation.

Source links:
- OpenAI: Prompt engineering
6. Q: How can a prompt use technical terminology without making the answer inaccessible?
A: Show answer
Use the terms that identify the concept accurately, then state how they should be explained. A prompt might say, “Use the terms qubit and superposition, define each on first use, and explain the distinction from a classical bit in plain language.” This preserves technical accuracy while making the answer readable. If the user is learning a subject, ask for a short glossary or an explanation of unfamiliar terms. If the audience is expert, avoid unnecessary definitions but retain precision. The aim is not to remove technical language; it is to control when it is introduced, how it is defined, and whether the reader can follow the reasoning.

Study instruction:
- Write a prompt that uses three specialist terms and instructs the model to define them without oversimplifying their meaning.

Key point:
- Technical terms should improve precision, not become unexplained obstacles.
- Specify whether definitions, acronyms, and plain-language paraphrases are needed.
7. Q: Why should a technical question be contextualized before it is submitted?
A: Show answer
Context tells the model which interpretation of the question is relevant. The same technical issue may have different answers depending on the operating environment, purpose, jurisdiction, user group, available resources, or system configuration. A question about a medical technology, for example, may need to distinguish diagnosis from treatment support and identify the patient population or care setting. An engineering question may depend on load, material, temperature, or safety requirements. Without context, the answer may be broadly correct but unsuitable for the user’s actual situation. Provide only the background that affects the answer, and separate established facts from uncertain or hypothetical details.

Study instruction:
- Add three relevant contextual facts to a broad technical question and explain how each fact changes the scope of the answer.

Key point:
- Context connects general information to the actual problem.
- Include conditions that materially affect the answer, not unrelated background.

Source links:
- OpenAI: Prompt engineering
8. Q: What kinds of context should be included in a prompt requesting technical information?
A: Show answer
Useful context may include the purpose of the inquiry, the system or process involved, the operating conditions, the audience, the relevant time period, geographic or regulatory setting, constraints, and what has already been tried. For a software question, include the language, version, environment, error message, and expected behavior. For an environmental question, identify the location, period, ecosystem, and impact being examined. For a healthcare information request, identify the general topic and the intended informational use while avoiding unnecessary personal data. The prompt should include context that changes the answer and omit details that merely lengthen the request.

Study instruction:
- Create a context checklist for one technical domain and mark which facts are essential, helpful, or irrelevant to the question.

Key point:
- Relevant context depends on the domain and decision being supported.
- More context is useful only when it changes interpretation or application.

Source links:
- OpenAI: Prompt engineering
9. Q: Why should assumptions be stated explicitly in a technical prompt?
A: Show answer
Assumptions define the conditions under which the answer is expected to apply. If they remain unstated, the model may silently choose values or conditions that do not match the user’s situation. A question about system performance may depend on workload, hardware, network conditions, and measurement method. A question about a scientific process may depend on environmental conditions or a simplified model. Stating assumptions allows the answer to be evaluated against the intended scenario and helps expose where conclusions would change. The prompt should label assumptions as assumptions rather than presenting them as verified facts. If an assumption is uncertain, ask the model to identify its effect on the conclusion.

Study instruction:
- List the assumptions behind a technical question and mark which are confirmed, provisional, or unknown.

Key point:
- Explicit assumptions reduce hidden interpretation choices.
- Keep assumptions distinct from established facts and evidence.
10. Q: How can a user clarify assumptions without making the prompt unnecessarily long?
A: Show answer
State only the assumptions that materially affect the answer, preferably in a short labeled list. For example: “Assume a small office network, fewer than 50 users, and no dedicated security team.” This is more useful than a long narrative that mixes background facts with conditions. If the assumptions are tentative, say so and ask the model to explain how the answer changes if they are false. A prompt can also request that any additional assumptions be listed before the conclusion. This prevents the model from silently filling gaps with convenient but unsupported conditions and gives the user a clear opportunity to correct them.

Study instruction:
- Rewrite a paragraph of background into three concise assumptions and one question about what changes if an assumption is wrong.

Key point:
- Use concise, labeled assumptions that affect the answer.
- Ask the model to expose any assumptions it must add.

Source links:
- OpenAI: Prompt engineering
11. Q: What should a prompt request when important context or assumptions are missing?
A: Show answer
Ask the model to identify the missing information and distinguish what can be answered safely from what depends on clarification. A useful instruction is: “If a missing condition would materially change the answer, list it and explain the alternatives rather than silently assuming one.” For low-risk questions, the model may provide a conditional answer under clearly stated assumptions. For high-impact technical or professional decisions, the user should supply the missing facts or consult a qualified source before acting. This approach prevents a confident answer from hiding uncertainty. It also makes the response more useful by showing which additional information would narrow the conclusion.

Study instruction:
- Take an under-specified question and identify the two missing facts most likely to change the answer; write a prompt that requests conditional treatment.

Key point:
- Missing context should be surfaced, not silently invented.
- Conditional answers must state the conditions on which they depend.

Source links:
- OpenAI: Prompt engineering
12. Q: Why should a user request sources or references when asking for factual information?
A: Show answer
References allow the user to inspect the basis for factual claims, check definitions, confirm dates, and investigate disagreements. They are particularly valuable when the subject is technical, contested, changing, or consequential. A prompt can request primary sources, official documentation, peer-reviewed research, standards, or other authorities appropriate to the domain. However, asking for references does not guarantee that every citation is genuine or that the source supports the claim. The user must still verify the source and the connection between the source and the statement. References are a route to independent checking, not a substitute for it.

Study instruction:
- For one factual question, specify the type of authority needed and explain why that source hierarchy fits the topic.

Key point:
- Sources make factual answers more traceable and independently checkable.
- A citation is useful only if it is real and supports the relevant claim.

- OpenAI: Prompt engineering
13. Q: How should a prompt ask for useful sources rather than a decorative list of references?
A: Show answer
Specify the source standard and the role references should play. For example, request official technical documentation for product behavior, a recognized standards body for a standard, or peer-reviewed studies for research findings. Ask the model to connect each important claim to the source that supports it and to distinguish primary evidence from commentary. If a source is unavailable, the response should say so rather than invent a title or URL. A short annotated list may be more useful than a long bibliography because it explains what each source establishes. The prompt should also state whether the user needs publication dates, version numbers, or access dates.

Study instruction:
- Draft a source instruction for a technical topic that names the preferred authority, citation detail, and what to do when a source cannot be verified.

Key point:
- Specify source quality and claim-to-source attribution.
- A smaller, relevant source set is better than an unverified reference list.

- OpenAI: Prompt engineering
14. Q: How can a user verify whether a cited source actually supports the answer?
A: Show answer
Open the source and compare its actual content with the claim attributed to it. Check the author or issuing organization, title, publication or revision date, version, scope, and whether the cited passage addresses the same question. A source may be authoritative but still irrelevant to a particular claim. For example, a general overview may not support a precise numerical threshold or a claim about a specific system version. Verify that the URL works and that the document has not been superseded. If the claim is broader than the evidence, narrow the wording or find stronger support. Never treat the presence of a hyperlink as proof of accuracy.

Study instruction:
- Select one cited claim and record the exact passage, source date, and limits of what the passage establishes.

Key point:
- Verify both the source’s authenticity and its relevance to the claim.
- Do not infer more than the cited evidence supports.

- OpenAI: Prompt engineering
15. Q: What does it mean to direct the focus of a technical question?
A: Show answer
Directing focus means identifying the specific component, mechanism, comparison, or decision the answer should address. A broad question such as “How do solar panels work?” could produce a general explanation of the entire technology. A focused question such as “What role do photovoltaic cells play in converting light into electrical energy?” narrows the task to one mechanism. A focused prompt can still request background, but it tells the model which issue deserves most attention. This reduces the chance of a long answer that is broadly relevant yet fails to explain the point the user actually needs to understand.

Study instruction:
- Turn a broad subject into three focused questions, each targeting a different component or mechanism.

Key point:
- Focus identifies the exact issue to be explained.
- A broad topic should not obscure the specific question being asked.
16. Q: How can a broad question be rewritten as a precise technical question?
A: Show answer
Identify the key concept, the relationship or mechanism of interest, and the boundary of the requested explanation. Replace “tell me about batteries” with a question about how lithium-ion cells store and release energy, or replace “explain networks” with a question about how routing decisions move packets between networks. Add context if the answer depends on a particular application or operating condition. Avoid narrowing the question so much that the necessary explanation becomes impossible; a mechanism may need a short description of its surrounding system. The goal is a clear center of attention with enough context to explain why it matters.

Study instruction:
- Rewrite three broad questions using the pattern “Explain [specific mechanism] in [context], focusing on [desired aspect].”

Key point:
- Name the concept and the relationship that matter.
- Add a boundary that prevents unnecessary topic drift.

17. Q: What does the chapter’s photovoltaic-cell example demonstrate about question focus?
A: Show answer
The example changes “How do solar panels work?” into a question about the role of photovoltaic cells in solar panel technology. The revised version directs attention to a component and its function rather than asking for a broad overview of an entire system. This makes the likely answer more relevant to someone who needs to understand the conversion mechanism. The example also shows that a good technical prompt often uses a precise noun phrase and a clear relationship: the user wants to know what role one component plays in a larger technology. The prompt can then add a desired level of detail or request a comparison if needed.

Study instruction:
- Apply the same narrowing method to another technology by identifying one component and asking what function it performs in the larger system.

Key point:
- A component-focused question is often more useful than a broad topic question.
- Ask about the role or mechanism that connects the component to the system.
18. Q: Why should complex technical questions be segmented into smaller parts?
A: Show answer
Complex questions often contain several different tasks: defining concepts, explaining mechanisms, comparing alternatives, evaluating evidence, and recommending an approach. Asking for all of them in one unstructured sentence can lead to omissions or a poorly organized answer. Segmentation turns the inquiry into manageable subquestions and makes it easier to see whether each part has been addressed. It also reveals dependencies: a comparison may require definitions first, and a recommendation may require criteria and evidence. The user should segment the question according to its logic, not simply create many small questions that repeat the same point.

Study instruction:
- Break a complex technical inquiry into four distinct tasks and arrange them in the order needed for a coherent answer.

Key point:
- Segmentation reduces omissions and improves answer organization.
- Divide by meaningful tasks and dependencies, not arbitrary sentence length.

- OpenAI: Prompt engineering
19. Q: How should a complex technical inquiry be divided into manageable subquestions?
A: Show answer
Separate the inquiry into stages such as definitions, operating principles, inputs and outputs, practical applications, risks, and evaluation. For a technology comparison, ask what each option is, how it works, what conditions it requires, where it performs well, and what limitations apply. For a research topic, separate the question, evidence, method, findings, and limitations. Each subquestion should have a distinct purpose and should not simply restate the previous one. If the final answer needs to integrate the parts, say so explicitly and request a concluding synthesis based on the earlier analysis.

Study instruction:
- Create a subquestion map for one complex topic and label each question with its distinct purpose.

Key point:
- Subquestions should divide the reasoning into non-overlapping tasks.
- Request a synthesis when the parts must support one overall conclusion.
20. Q: Why can enumerated or bulleted questions improve a multi-part prompt?
A: Show answer
Enumeration makes the requested tasks visible and gives the model a clear structure to follow. A numbered list can show sequence, while bullets can group related questions that do not require a strict order. This is useful when the user needs several facts, comparisons, or explanations and wants to reduce the risk that one will be overlooked. Each item should be phrased clearly and should request a distinct result. If the questions are dependent, number them in logical order. If they are independent, say that each should be answered separately. Enumeration improves coverage, but it does not guarantee accuracy; the resulting answers still need review.

Study instruction:
- Convert a paragraph containing five requests into a numbered list and check that every item asks for a different deliverable.

Key point:
- Lists make multi-part requirements easier to track.
- Clear wording and logical order remain necessary.

- OpenAI: Prompt engineering
21. Q: How should the order of subquestions be chosen in a technical prompt?
A: Show answer
Put foundational questions before questions that depend on them. Define the system and its terminology first, then explain the mechanism, compare alternatives, examine risks, and draw conclusions. If the task is diagnostic, begin with observed symptoms and operating conditions before exploring possible causes and tests. If it is evaluative, state the criteria before asking which option performs best. A logical order reduces the risk that the answer will rely on concepts not yet explained. It also helps the user detect missing reasoning steps. The prompt can explicitly request that the answer follow the same sequence as the questions.

Study instruction:
- Reorder a mixed list of technical questions into a sequence from definition to explanation, evaluation, and conclusion.

Key point:
- Order subquestions according to logical and evidential dependencies.
- Define the criteria before asking for a judgment or recommendation.
22. Q: Why should a technical prompt specify the desired answer format?
A: Show answer
Format determines how information is organized and how easily the user can apply it. A narrative explanation may be best for a mechanism, a comparison table for alternatives, a checklist for a procedure, and a numbered sequence for ordered steps. If the user needs information for a report, presentation, database, or decision record, the structure may be important to downstream use. A prompt should specify the format and any essential fields, headings, or ordering rules. It should not request a table merely because tables look organized; the format should make the information clearer. If several formats are needed, ask for separate sections rather than mixing incompatible structures.

Study instruction:
- Choose the best format for an explanation, comparison, procedure, and evidence summary, and justify each choice.

Key point:
- Format should serve the user’s task and the structure of the information.
- State required headings, fields, and order when they matter.

- OpenAI: Prompt engineering
23. Q: How should a user choose between a paragraph, list, table, or step-by-step answer?
A: Show answer
Use paragraphs when relationships and reasoning need to be explained in continuous prose. Use bullets when the items are parallel and their order is not important. Use numbered steps when sequence matters, such as a procedure or troubleshooting workflow. Use a table when the reader needs to compare the same attributes across several items. A checklist is appropriate when the user must verify that conditions have been met. The prompt should also specify whether the answer needs an executive summary, technical detail, examples, or references. A format is successful when it helps the reader locate, compare, and apply the information without hiding important qualifications.

Study instruction:
- Present the same set of technical facts in prose, bullets, and a table; identify which format makes the key relationships easiest to see.

Key point:
- Select format based on the task: explain, list, sequence, compare, or verify.
- Good formatting improves usability but cannot repair weak content.
24. Q: How can a prompt specify a structured output without making the requested answer ambiguous?
A: Show answer
Name the required sections or fields and explain what belongs in each. For example, a technical explanation might require “definition, operating principle, practical example, limitations, and sources.” A comparison table might require one row per option and columns for purpose, requirements, strengths, limitations, and suitable use cases. State whether missing information should be marked as unknown rather than inferred. If the format is strict, specify whether introductory prose or a conclusion is allowed. Clear structure reduces omissions and makes the output easier to review, but the fields must be relevant to the topic rather than copied from an unrelated template.

Study instruction:
- Draft a structured-output instruction with five fields and define the evidence needed for each field.

Key point:
- A structure is reliable when every field has a clear meaning.
- Tell the model how to represent missing or uncertain information.

- OpenAI: Prompt engineering
25. Q: How can a prompt improve the accuracy and relevance of a technical answer?
A: Show answer
Accuracy and relevance are supported by a focused question, appropriate terminology, sufficient context, explicit assumptions, a suitable detail level, and a clear evidence standard. The user should identify the exact issue and specify which aspects are in scope. The prompt can ask the model to separate established facts from interpretation, state uncertainty, and avoid inventing missing details. It should also request sources that match the domain and the type of claim. These measures improve the conditions for a useful answer, but they do not guarantee correctness. The user must still verify consequential claims against authoritative evidence and check whether the answer applies to the actual situation.

Study instruction:
- Evaluate a technical prompt against focus, terminology, context, assumptions, evidence, and uncertainty; revise any missing element.

Key point:
- Prompt design improves the conditions for accuracy but does not prove the answer is correct.
- Independent verification remains essential for consequential information.

26. Q: When should a prompt specify the date or version relevant to a factual answer?
A: Show answer
Include a date or version when the subject changes over time or depends on a particular release, regulation, study period, or operating environment. Software behavior may differ across versions; technical standards may be revised; scientific findings may develop; and public statistics may refer to different years. A prompt can ask for the latest available information as of a stated date or request a comparison between two specified versions. It should also ask the model to identify the publication date of important sources. Without a time boundary, an answer may combine current and outdated information or present a historical condition as if it still applies.

Study instruction:
- Add a date or version boundary to three changing-information questions and specify what source date would be acceptable.

Key point:
- Time and version can be essential parts of a technical question.
- Ask for source dates when information may become outdated.

27. Q: How should a prompt ask the model to communicate uncertainty and limitations?
A: Show answer
Request that uncertain claims be labeled, that important limitations be explained, and that missing evidence not be replaced with guesses. A useful instruction is: “Separate well-established findings from tentative interpretations, identify material uncertainties, and state what additional evidence would change the conclusion.” This encourages a calibrated answer rather than one that sounds equally confident about every point. The prompt can also ask for alternative explanations where evidence is incomplete. In technical domains, limitations may arise from sample size, measurement methods, model assumptions, environmental conditions, or version differences. The user should treat uncertainty as information to evaluate, not as a defect to conceal.

Study instruction:
- Add an uncertainty instruction to a factual prompt and identify three kinds of limitation the answer should disclose.

Key point:
- A trustworthy answer distinguishes confidence from uncertainty.
- Limitations explain where a conclusion may not apply.

28. Q: How should a user select primary or authoritative sources for a technical question?
A: Show answer
Match the source to the claim and domain. Official standards and documentation are usually appropriate for requirements and product behavior; government or intergovernmental bodies may be suitable for official statistics and regulatory information; peer-reviewed research can support scientific findings; and original datasets may be necessary for quantitative analysis. A secondary explanation can help with context, but it should not replace the primary source when a precise rule or technical specification is at issue. The prompt should name the preferred source type and ask the model to disclose when no suitable primary source is available. Authority depends on the question, not simply on a source’s reputation.

Study instruction:
- For one technical question, identify the source type that would best establish the answer and explain why a general blog would be insufficient.

Key point:
- Choose sources according to the type of claim being made.
- Prefer primary or official material for specifications, standards, and authoritative facts.

29. Q: How can a prompt ask for comparison of sources that disagree?
A: Show answer
Ask the model to identify the exact point of disagreement, compare the sources’ dates, methods, definitions, scope, and evidence, and explain whether the apparent conflict can be reconciled. Two sources may report different results because they study different populations, use different measurement methods, or refer to different periods. The prompt should not instruct the model to choose one source merely because it supports a preferred conclusion. It should ask which evidence is strongest for the specific question and what remains unresolved. If the sources cannot be reconciled, the answer should describe the disagreement and avoid presenting a false consensus.

Study instruction:
- Compare two sources on one disputed claim and record differences in date, scope, method, and evidence before drawing a conclusion.

Key point:
- Source disagreement requires analysis of scope and method, not simple vote-counting.
- Preserve unresolved differences when the evidence does not settle them.

- OpenAI: Prompt engineering
30. Q: What is the difference between requesting a source and requesting an explanation of the evidence behind a claim?
A: Show answer
Requesting a source asks where information can be checked; requesting an explanation of the evidence asks why that information supports the conclusion. A link alone does not show whether a study was well designed, whether a technical document applies to the correct version, or whether a statistic measures the concept being discussed. A stronger prompt asks the model to summarize the relevant evidence, identify its limitations, and connect it to the claim. The user can then inspect the original source and determine whether the interpretation is justified. This distinction is important when the answer will inform a technical decision or a public factual statement.

Study instruction:
- For one claim, request both a source link and a brief explanation of the evidence that supports the claim; check whether the explanation matches the source.

Key point:
- A reference identifies where to look; evidence analysis explains what the source establishes.
- Check the reasoning that connects evidence to conclusion.
31. Q: How can a prompt distinguish facts, interpretations, and recommendations?
A: Show answer
Ask for separate treatment of what is directly established, what is inferred from the evidence, and what action is being recommended. Facts should be supported by sources or clearly identified data. Interpretations should explain the reasoning and any assumptions. Recommendations should state the criteria and trade-offs that lead to them. Mixing these categories can make a subjective judgment appear to be an objective requirement. A structured answer can use separate headings or labels, but the key is conceptual separation. This is especially important when the user needs to explain a conclusion to others or when the evidence does not support a single inevitable decision.

Study instruction:
- Take a technical answer and label each sentence as fact, interpretation, or recommendation; revise any sentence that blurs the categories.

Key point:
- Facts, inferences, and advice have different evidential status.
- Make the reasoning behind recommendations visible.

Source links:
- OpenAI: Prompt engineering
32. Q: How should a prompt request calculations or quantitative analysis in a technical explanation?
A: Show answer
State the data, units, assumptions, method, and required precision. Ask the model to show the formula or reasoning steps that are necessary for checking the result, and to identify any missing inputs. If estimates are acceptable, label them as estimates and state the assumptions that drive them. If exactness matters, verify the calculation independently using a reliable method or tool. The prompt should also distinguish measured data from illustrative figures. A numerical answer without units, context, or method can be misleading even when the arithmetic is correct. Ask for a sensitivity explanation when the result changes materially with one or more assumptions.

Study instruction:
- Write a quantitative prompt that includes units, assumptions, method, and an instruction for handling missing values.

Key point:
- Quantitative answers require transparent inputs, units, and assumptions.
- Verify important calculations independently.
33. Q: How can a prompt request a technical explanation that is both accurate and understandable?
A: Show answer
Ask for a layered explanation. Begin with a concise definition or overview, then explain the mechanism using precise terminology, and finish with an example that shows how the concept operates in practice. The prompt can request that technical terms be defined on first use and that analogies be labeled as simplifications. It should also ask the model to identify where the analogy stops being accurate. This approach avoids two common problems: a simplified answer that loses essential distinctions and a specialist answer that assumes too much background. The user can specify which level should receive the most emphasis based on the intended audience.

Study instruction:
- Ask for a three-layer explanation of one technical concept and identify which sentence provides the definition, mechanism, and practical example.

Key point:
- Layered explanations combine accessibility with technical precision.
- Analogies help only when their limitations are made clear.

34. Q: What should a prompt include when asking for a comparison such as quantum computing versus classical computing?
A: Show answer
Identify the exact dimensions to compare and ask for definitions before conclusions. The chapter’s example focuses on quantum bits and binary bits. A stronger prompt can ask for the basic representation of information, the role of superposition, measurement, typical computational use cases, current practical limitations, and common misconceptions. It should avoid implying that a quantum computer is simply a faster classical computer or that it automatically solves every difficult problem. Ask the model to distinguish theoretical capabilities from present-day systems and to support technical claims with reliable sources. This creates a more balanced comparison than a question that asks only which technology is better.

Study instruction:
- Build a comparison table for two technical approaches and specify the dimensions, limitations, and misconceptions that must be addressed.

Key point:
- Comparisons need shared criteria and accurate definitions.
- Separate theoretical potential from demonstrated practical capability.

35. Q: How can a prompt about AI in healthcare avoid producing an incomplete or misleading answer?
A: Show answer
Define the healthcare application, the users affected, the type of data involved, and the decision the system supports. The chapter’s example narrows the topic to diagnostics and patient-data analysis while asking for existing concerns. A more complete prompt can request benefits, validation requirements, privacy risks, bias, explainability, human oversight, clinical workflow, and limits of generalization. It should ask for current, authoritative evidence and distinguish research prototypes from systems used in practice. It should not assume that faster analysis automatically means better care. If the question concerns a real patient or clinical decision, the prompt should seek general information and direct the user to qualified clinical judgment rather than treating an AI response as a diagnosis.

Study instruction:
- Rewrite the healthcare example to include application scope, evidence standard, risks, and the distinction between research and clinical use.

Key point:
- High-impact technical topics need context, evidence, and explicit limitations.

36. Q: How can a prompt about deforestation request relevant environmental data without inviting unsupported statistics?
A: Show answer
Specify the geographic area, time period, environmental effects, and type of evidence required. The chapter’s example focuses on the Amazon rainforest, biodiversity, climate change, and relevant data or studies. A stronger prompt asks for the source, publication date, measurement method, geographic scope, and uncertainty associated with each statistic. It should distinguish observed change from modeled projections and avoid combining figures that use incompatible definitions. If data are unavailable for the requested period, the answer should say so rather than fill the gap with an estimate presented as fact. This makes quantitative information traceable and helps prevent misleading comparisons.

Study instruction:
- Draft a data-focused environmental prompt that requires source dates, geographic scope, units, and a note about measurement limitations.

- Data requests need a defined place, period, measure, and source standard.

37. Q: How should a prompt ask for relevant data or studies on a technical topic?
A: Show answer
Specify what kind of evidence is useful and how it should be summarized. The prompt may ask for peer-reviewed studies, official datasets, standards, or technical reports, depending on the question. Request the study population or system, method, sample or data period, main finding, limitations, and publication date where relevant. Ask the model to distinguish a study’s result from its authors’ interpretation and from any broader conclusion drawn by the response. If no suitable study is found, the answer should disclose that limitation. This prevents a generic list of papers from being mistaken for a meaningful evidence review.

Study instruction:
- Design a study-summary format with fields for source, method, scope, finding, limitation, and relevance to the question.

Key point:
- Evidence is useful when its method and scope are understood.

38. Q: What is false precision, and how can a technical prompt reduce the risk of it?
A: Show answer
False precision occurs when an answer presents a number, threshold, date, or categorical conclusion more precisely than the evidence justifies. A model may provide an exact-looking figure even when the source is approximate, the measurement method varies, or the underlying data are incomplete. A prompt can reduce this risk by asking for units, ranges, confidence or uncertainty where appropriate, the source date, and the method used. It can also instruct the model not to invent exact values when only qualitative information is available. The user should distinguish an illustrative example from an empirical result and verify any number that may influence a decision.

Study instruction:
- Review a numerical answer and identify whether its precision is supported by the source, method, and stated uncertainty.

Key point:
- Precision in presentation must not exceed precision in evidence.
- Use ranges or qualify estimates when exact values are not justified.
39. Q: How should a prompt handle conflicting or incomplete technical information?
A: Show answer
Ask the model to identify the conflict, describe what is known, and state which additional evidence would resolve the uncertainty. It should not silently choose one account or combine incompatible values. Incomplete information may require a conditional answer, a list of plausible interpretations, or a request for further context. The prompt can specify that the model should mark unknown fields explicitly and avoid filling them with invented facts. For technical systems, the missing information may be a version, configuration, measurement, or environmental condition. For research topics, it may be a gap in data or a limitation in study design. Clear handling of uncertainty protects the user from false certainty.

Study instruction:
- Create a prompt for a case with two conflicting sources and one missing fact; ask for a conditional conclusion and the evidence needed to decide.

Key point:
- Conflicts and gaps should be exposed and explained.
- Do not convert incomplete evidence into a definitive conclusion.

- OpenAI: Prompt engineering
40. Q: How can a user request both a concise answer and enough technical detail to understand the issue?
A: Show answer
Ask for a layered output with a short answer first and supporting detail afterward. For example, request a two-sentence summary, followed by the mechanism, assumptions, evidence, limitations, and one practical example. This allows a busy reader to understand the conclusion quickly while preserving the material needed for deeper review. The prompt should specify which details are essential and which may be omitted. If the answer is intended for a report, ask for a separate executive summary and technical appendix. Concision should remove repetition, not remove conditions or caveats that materially affect the conclusion.

Study instruction:
- Ask for a short summary and a detailed explanation of the same topic; compare whether the summary preserves the important qualifications.

Key point:
- Layered answers support both quick reading and technical review.
- Concision should not remove essential assumptions or limitations.

- OpenAI: Prompt engineering
41. Q: How can examples improve the quality of a technical or informational prompt?
A: Show answer
Examples show the model the kind of input, reasoning, or output pattern the user expects. A sample table can clarify required columns; a sample explanation can demonstrate the desired depth; and a sample citation can show how sources should be presented. Examples are particularly useful when the requested format is unusual or difficult to describe abstractly. They should be representative rather than narrowly tailored to one answer, and they should not contain incorrect facts that the model may reproduce. If examples are illustrative, label them as such. A prompt can also ask the model to follow the pattern without copying the sample’s content or assumptions.

Study instruction:
- Add one good example and one boundary example to a prompt, then state what each teaches about the expected output.

Key point:
- Examples demonstrate patterns that instructions may describe imperfectly.
- Use accurate, varied examples and distinguish form from content.

- OpenAI: Prompt engineering
42. Q: What is the difference between asking for an example and asking for evidence?
A: Show answer
An example illustrates how a concept might work; evidence supports a factual claim about what is true. A hypothetical example can clarify a mechanism without proving that the mechanism operates in every real situation. A study, standard, measurement, or official record may provide evidence, but it still has a defined scope and limitations. A prompt should make clear whether it wants an illustrative scenario, an empirical finding, or both. If examples are invented for teaching, ask that they be labeled as hypothetical. If factual evidence is required, request the source and the method by which the claim was established.

Study instruction:
- Classify five statements as illustration or evidence and identify what source would be needed to support each factual claim.

Key point:
- Examples explain; evidence substantiates.
- Label hypothetical scenarios so they are not mistaken for real observations.
43. Q: What is a practical template for a technical or informational prompt?
A: Show answer
A reusable template is: “Explain [specific question or mechanism] for [audience]. The context is [relevant background]. Assume [explicit conditions] and do not assume [excluded conditions]. Focus on [scope] and exclude [out-of-scope topics]. Provide [desired detail level] in [format]. Define technical terms on first use. Support important factual claims with [preferred source types], include source dates where relevant, and distinguish facts from interpretation. Identify uncertainty, limitations, and missing information. If a conclusion depends on an unstated assumption, state it rather than guessing.” Adapt the fields to the subject. A prompt for a quick definition does not need the same structure as a technical evaluation or evidence review.

Study instruction:
- Complete the template for a technical topic and remove any field that does not materially improve the answer.

Key point:
- A strong factual prompt makes scope, context, assumptions, detail, format, and evidence explicit.
- Templates should be adapted to the complexity of the question.

- OpenAI: Prompt engineering
44. Q: How should a user evaluate the quality of an AI-generated technical or informational answer?
A: Show answer
Check whether the answer addresses the exact question, uses terms accurately, respects the stated context and assumptions, provides the requested level of detail, and follows the requested format. Then inspect the evidence: are the sources genuine, current enough, authoritative for the claim, and correctly interpreted? Review numerical values, units, dates, and comparisons for consistency. Look for missing caveats, unsupported certainty, conflation of facts with recommendations, and claims that extend beyond the cited evidence. Finally, ask whether the answer is useful for the stated purpose. A polished response can still be wrong, incomplete, or irrelevant, so quality control must examine both content and provenance.

Study instruction:
- Use a review checklist covering relevance, terminology, context, assumptions, evidence, uncertainty, format, and practical usefulness.

Key point:
- Fluency and formatting are not proof of factual correctness.
- Verify claims, sources, and applicability before relying on the answer.

Source links:
- OpenAI: Prompt engineering
45. Q: What are the most important principles for writing technical and informational prompts?
A: Show answer
Define the exact focus, use precise terminology, state the desired level of detail, and provide the context and assumptions that determine how the question should be interpreted. Break complex inquiries into logical subquestions, use lists when several tasks must be covered, and specify a format that makes the answer useful. For factual claims, request appropriate sources and ask the model to disclose uncertainty, limitations, and missing information. Treat examples as illustrations unless they are supported by evidence, and verify references before relying on them. The chapter’s central lesson is that a well-structured prompt improves relevance and makes an answer easier to evaluate, but responsibility for checking consequential information remains with the user.

Study instruction:
- Apply the chapter’s full checklist to one real technical question, then verify the answer against at least one appropriate primary source.

Key point:
- Accuracy begins with a well-scoped, contextualized, evidence-aware question.
- Prompting improves answer quality, while verification protects against error.

- OpenAI: Prompt engineering
